⏺ Added and pushed. The new section at the end of the README:

  ▎ Status
  ▎
  ▎ As of 2026-11-13:
  ▎
  ▎ - Built: the storage. A database is a log of commits in S3, with a local 
  ▎ SQLite cache. One instance at a time holds a lease on it. Commits, recovery 
  ▎ after a crash, and replay of the log work. They are tested in a 
  ▎ deterministic simulation and against a real S3-compatible bucket.
  ▎ - Not built: the SQL. There is no parser, no engine, and no public API yet, 
  ▎ so the database cannot be used.
  ▎ - Next: the first slice of SQL in the roadmap: create table, insert, select,
  ▎ and drop table for one integer column, checked against SQLite.
  ▎
  ▎ What is open in detail is in docs/todo.md.

  I put it below everything, as you said, which places it after the "Made with
  sparkles" footer and the contact line. Say if you'd rather have it above those
  two.

  To keep it current, I added a line to the README rule in AGENTS.md: the
  section is updated when a piece of the project is merged, stays at three short
  points, and the details stay in the todo.

✻ Cooked for 2m 17s · done Friday 9:04 PM

› Message from fabrik:builder
  ## Report: a commit object that the store can never take

  The branch is `worktree-agent-a7c599482bc144ef6`. It is one commit, `87939e3`,
  on top of `main` (`186ccdb`). Nothing is pushed.

  A commit whose commit object is too large for the store now fails at once,
  with an error the caller can test for. Before, the core retried the upload
  until the caller's context ended. `make test`, `make lint` and `make test-asan
  FUZZTIME=1s` (in Docker) pass.

  ### Decisions
  - **`store.ErrTooLarge`** is the fifth "not applied" error. It is documented
  in `/internal/store/store.go`: in the package comment, in its own doc ("a
  write of the same content will never be applied"), and in the docs of `Create`
  and `Swap`.
  - **`s3store.ErrTooLarge` is gone.** `s3store` returns `store.ErrTooLarge`, so
  callers have one name to test. `MaxSize` stays.
  - **`s3store` also maps a store's own `400 EntityTooLarge` answer to
  `store.ErrTooLarge`.** This was not in the brief; the review found it was
  needed. Without it, a store whose limit is below 5 GiB would still be retried
  until the context ended. This rests on AWS's error-code list only; no store
  was seen to send that answer. The README says so.
  - **New request outcome in `s3store`:** `too_large`, logged at Warn, for both
  the client's own check and the store's answer. Before, a too-large write
  showed as `not_sent`, the same as a bad key.
  - **`core.ErrTooLarge`** is in `/internal/core/errors.go` and documented as
  not temporary.
    - `Tx.upload` returns `fmt.Errorf("%w: %v", ErrTooLarge, err)` at the first
  such refusal, with no retry. It wraps the core's error and not the store's.
    - `Tx.Commit` already did the rest for a failed upload: it rolls back the
  cache, closes the spool file (which deletes it), ends the transaction, and
  logs a Warn event with outcome `not_committed` through `LogAttrs(ctx, ...)`.
    - The docs of `Tx.Commit` and `Tx.upload` name the new error.
  - **The manifest case:** a manifest write refused as too large is treated as
  an unknown outcome, like any other error.
    - `refusal` in `/internal/core/lease.go` leaves `store.ErrTooLarge` out on
  purpose. The comment says why: only a misconfigured store refuses a manifest
  of a few hundred bytes, and treating a write that was not applied as unknown
  is never wrong, only slower.
    - It needs no new code path.
    - Treating it as a refusal would make renewals and takes retry every 100 ms
  (`retrySoon`) for as long as the store is misconfigured. Unknown backs off
  instead.
  - **The size limit in `simstore`** is `Options.MaxSize int` (bytes, 0 means no
  limit). The new `Client.checkSize` refuses a larger create or swap with
  `store.ErrTooLarge` before sending it, and traces it. It draws no random
  number and counts no request, so seeds and traces are unchanged when it is
  off. The change is small and stays out of `Faults` and the in-flight code, so
  it should merge cleanly with the other builder's branch.
  - **`storetest` is unchanged.** A general case would need the suite to know
  each store's limit.

  ### How the tests show it
  - **`TestTx_Commit_tooLarge`** (`/internal/core/tx_test.go`) runs against a
  `simstore` with a 2 KiB limit. A transaction of 1000 rows (about 12 KB) is
  committed under a context of one hour. It checks:
    - the error wraps `core.ErrTooLarge` and not `ErrOutcomeUnknown`,
  `ErrNoLease`, `ErrStopped` or `DeadlineExceeded`;
    - the context has not ended;
    - exactly one upload was sent;
    - the transaction is over (`ErrTxDone`);
    - there is one `not_committed` commit event at Warn with `attempts=1`;
    - the manifest head and the cache are unchanged, and `Wait` still succeeds;
    - the next transaction commits, and the log has exactly two commits.
  - **Red/green:** before the fix, the test failed with the old behaviour:
  `upload of the commit object …: context deadline exceeded, after: simstore:
  11932 bytes, more than 2048: …`.
  - **Broken by hand:** I changed the handling to retry once (`case
  errors.Is(err, store.ErrTooLarge) && *attempts >= 2:`). The test failed with
  `Expected "1", but got "2" … the upload was sent again`. I then restored the
  code.
  - **`TestLease_tooLarge`** (`/internal/core/lease_test.go`) uses a 16-byte
  limit. The instance keeps asking for the lease (at least 2 attempts to create
  the manifest), does not stop (`d.Err()` is nil), and refuses reads with
  `ErrNoLease`. It does not tell "unknown" from "refusal"; that would mean
  counting attempts against the back-off.
  - **`TestClient_tooLarge`** (simstore) shows a refused create and swap, a
  create exactly at the limit, and that the refused writes are not applied, even
  with faults on that would otherwise make a sent write fail or land late.
  - **`TestClient_answers`** (s3store) has a new row for `400 EntityTooLarge` on
  create and on swap.
  - **Repeat runs:** the new tests passed with `-count=50`, with `-race
  -count=50`, and with `-race -count=200 -cpu 1,4` while `make test` ran
  alongside.
  - **Caveat on asan:** `make test-asan` ran before the review fixes. Those
  fixes touch no code that waits, and the core tests passed again under `-race
  -count=50` after them.

  ### Docs
  - The bullet is deleted from `/docs/todo.md`.
  - `/internal/store/s3store/README.md` uses the new name and has the
  `EntityTooLarge` sentence.
  - `/internal/core/README.md` has two new bullets under "The commit": the clean
  failure, and the manifest case.
  - `README.md` and `docs/spec.md` are untouched.
  - The diary is `/docs/diary/2026-11-13-commit-too-large.md`, three steps,
  committed with the code.

  ### Self-review
  The code-review skill (medium) found eight things. I acted on four:
  - the `EntityTooLarge` mapping;
  - the `too_large` outcome;
  - `attempt.definite` in the `simstore` test model now knows the fifth error;
  - a shorter error message from `Tx.upload`.

  I left four:
  - **The manifest case as a refusal:** this is decision 1 below.
  - **A sentinel leaking through `remaining` in `s3store`:** the claim is wrong,
  since it formats errors with `%v`.
  - **A `storetest` case:** it does not fit the suite, as above.
  - **`simstore` reads the content before checking its size:** fixing it would
  touch more of `simstore` while the other builder is changing it.

  ### Surprises
  - The schema has only `TYPE_INT`, so the test cannot use a blob and needs many
  rows.
  - A size limit alone cannot make a store accept the lease and then refuse a
  commit's manifest swap, because a small commit object is smaller than the
  manifest. So the manifest case is tested on taking the lease.

  ### For you to decide
  1. **The manifest case: unknown (what I built) or refusal?** With refusal, a
  commit whose swap is refused as too large would return "not committed" instead
  of `ErrOutcomeUnknown`, and the instance would not wait unresolved. The cost
  is a retry every 100 ms on renewals and takes. Either is safe.
  2. **Proposed sentence for `docs/spec.md`, if Markus wants it:** "A
  transaction whose commit object is larger than the store takes in one write is
  not committed, and its commit fails at once with an error that says so; on S3
  the limit is 5 GiB."

✻ Cogitated for 27s · done Friday 9:19 PM

› Message from general-purpose
  The note is written and committed on branch `worktree-agent-a95f60c048b3c6625`
  (commit 560cc9a), not pushed. It is `docs/research/sql-front-end.md`, about
  395 lines, plus one entry in `docs/research/README.md`. The secrets check is
  clean.

  **How I got the facts.** I didn't trust the Homebrew shell, whose compile
  options differ. Instead I built throwaway C probes against the vendored
  amalgamation with `options.h`, opened the way `sqlite.Open` opens (EXRESCODE).
  I read `parse.y` and `lemon.c` at tag version-3.54.1, and the Go 1.27.3
  `database/sql` source. Three research agents surveyed the parsers, the testing
  practice and the APIs; I checked their key claims. The probes are in
  `/private/tmp/claude-502/-Users-maragubot-Developer-s4ql--claude-worktrees-obj
  ect-store/f1343515-adca-4ca0-9eb0-f507768f8fc7/scratchpad/fe/probe/`.

  **1. Tokenizer.** SQLite's tokenizer is one byte-level function,
  `sqlite3GetToken`, with quirks a Go copy must keep:
  - A vertical tab is legal only after other whitespace.
  - A byte order mark counts as whitespace only at the start of a token.
  - Every byte from 0x80 up is an identifier character, so a no-break space
  (U+00A0) is part of a name.
  - Digit separators like `1_` are checked later, and that error carries offset
  -1.
  - An unterminated `/*` comment is accepted.

  s4ql's restrictions don't shrink the tokenizer, because brackets and backticks
  must still be found as tokens to be rejected.
  - **Oracle:** a C test program that includes `sqlite3.c` can call the private
  `sqlite3GetToken` directly. I did this, and it gives an exact token-by-token
  oracle for Go's fuzzer.
  - **Weak oracle:** `sqlite3_complete` uses a different scanner. `select 1;--x`
  counts as incomplete, which contradicts its own header doc.
  `sqlite3_incomplete` is new in 3.54.0.
  - **Evidence favours:** a tokenizer for all of SQLite's tokens, checked
  against `sqlite3GetToken`.

  **2. Parser.**
  - **Facts:**
    - `near "X"` is fixed by the grammar's language (inferred from LR theory,
  not proven). So a parser for slice 1 alone stops earlier than SQLite and
  cannot match SQLite's syntax errors on inputs outside the subset.
    - The order of errors is subtle. `sqlite3ErrorMsg` overwrites any earlier
  message, and the parse loop checks only between tokens. Measured: `... strict,
  bar, without rowid` reports the bad table option, but `(v1 foo ...) strict,
  without rowid, bar` reports `unknown datatype`.
    - `table X already exists` is raised mid-parse, so it wins over later
  faults.
  - **Biggest find:** `github.com/sqlc-dev/meyer`. It is an MIT, hand-written
  recursive-descent port of all of `parse.y` that claims SQLite's exact syntax
  messages and offsets on 21,326 cases. sqlc main switched to it on 2026-08-01.
  It has one author and seven commits, and pins 3.53.4. I checked that between
  3.53.4 and 3.54.1 `tokenize.c` is identical and `parse.y` differs only inside
  C action code. I did not run its tests.
  - **Others:**
    - rqlite/sql: Go-style errors, no digit separators.
    - modernc: transpiled C with no parser API.
    - ANTLR grammars: ANTLR's messages, and the Go runtime has had no release
  since 2024.
    - golemon: the author promises no support, and a 2022 attempt on `parse.y`
  was abandoned.
    - goyacc: has no `%fallback`.
  - **Evidence favours:** a full-grammar parser that s4ql owns, with a pass
  after parsing that rejects anything slice 1 does not list. meyer would serve
  as a reference or a starting point. A slice-1-only parser is the smaller start
  if Markus doesn't require SQLite's syntax messages for unsupported inputs.

  **3. Exact errors (run on 3.54.1).**

  | Case | Message | Primary / extended | At |
  |---|---|---|---|
  | Key inserted twice | `UNIQUE constraint failed: t1.v1` | 19 / 1555 | step |
  | Table exists | `table t1 already exists` | 1 / 1, offset 13 | prepare |
  | Missing table (select, insert, drop) | `no such table: t2` | 1 / 1 | prepare
  |

  How names appear:
  - `already exists` prints the name exactly as typed, quotes included: `table
  "t1" already exists`.
  - `no such table` prints the name as typed but without quotes, and keeps any
  schema prefix.
  - `UNIQUE` prints the stored names (`Foo.Bar`), whatever case the statement
  used.

  So the syntax tree must keep the raw token text of names.

  Slice 1's own shapes can also produce errors the roadmap doesn't list:
  - `table t1 has no column named v2`
  - `object name reserved for internal use: sqlite_x`
  - `table sqlite_master may not be dropped`
  - `cannot store REAL value in INT column t1.v1` (3091), for
  9223372036854775808
  - `hex literal too big`

  For most inputs s4ql will reject, SQLite gives no error at all: a second
  column, `integer`, `where`, `if exists`, missing `strict`. A few are SQLite
  errors too, such as `PRIMARY KEY missing` and `sqlite_` names.

  The note lays out three options for what s4ql promises to match, and how to
  mark "not supported" (a distinct error, a message that can't be SQLite's, the
  offset). The facts for that choice: matching codes alone can't tell `no such
  table` from `already exists`, because both are 1 / 1. And the docs promise no
  stable message text, but the version is pinned.

  **4. Testing.**
  - **musql** (`samyfodil/musql`) is a Go engine with mattn/go-sqlite3 as its
  oracle, which is s4ql's setup. It compares outcome kind, column names and
  typed values, and keeps "declined" separate from "wrong".
  - **Turso** runs `EXPLAIN` on both sides first and skips any statement either
  side can't prepare, so the two states never drift.
  - None of the surveyed projects compares error messages in live differential
  tests. Exact messages would make s4ql stricter than all of them, which a
  pinned in-process oracle allows.
  - The note sketches a slice-1 harness:
    - hand-written scripts for each measured case
    - a Hegel generator of slice-1 shapes
    - a mutator that changes one token, to check the "reject what is not listed"
  rule
    - tokenizer fuzzing against `sqlite3GetToken`
    - mined TCL corpora
    - table snapshots after each change and after replay from S3

  **5. API.**
  - **Retry hazard:** `database/sql` runs an operation up to three times when
  the driver returns `ErrBadConn` (`sql.go` 1574-1589). This covers
  `DB.ExecContext`, `QueryContext`, `BeginTx`, `Ping` and `Stmt.Exec`. An insert
  whose commit outcome is unknown, and `ErrNoLease`, must never wrap
  `ErrBadConn`.
  - **Commit context:** `driver.Tx.Commit()` takes no context. `sql.Tx.Commit`
  cancels its own derived context before calling the driver, but the driver's
  `BeginTx` gets the caller's original context, so the driver can keep it for
  `core.Tx.Commit`.
  - **What the driver can't carry:** `DB.Close` takes no context, and nothing in
  `database/sql` expresses the instance stopping.
  - **Other drivers:** none of the surveyed ones returns `ErrBadConn` from query
  paths.
  - **Agreement:** what Markus agreed on 2026-11-10 (`database/sql` as the main
  interface, `OpenDB` with a connector, an explicit `Register`) is only in the
  conversation export, not in `spec.md` or `todo.md`.
  - **Evidence favours:** the driver as the main interface, built as a thin
  adapter over an own API, the way ncruces is built.

  **6. Layers.**
  - The core's catalogue (`Tx.Table`/`Tables`) and its errors cover slice 1's
  cases, but the engine has to format SQLite's messages itself from the syntax
  tree.
  - `Tx.Upsert` replaces silently, so the engine must check the key first and
  raise `UNIQUE` itself.
  - SQLite's bytecode is not a stable interface by its own docs (opcode.html).
  Turso copies it and compares `EXPLAIN` by hand; go-mysql-server, chai, TiDB
  and ramsql all use iterator trees.
  - For slice 1 the two hardly differ.
  - The note lists four places where the oracle plugs in: tokens, statements,
  table state (the cache is SQLite too) and acceptance.

  **Surprising:**
  - meyer exists and claims SQLite-exact syntax errors in Go.
  - Error messages depend on whether several errors happen while the same token
  is handled.
  - `sqlite3_complete` contradicts its own doc.
  - SQLite accepts `insert into t (v1, v1) values (1, 2)`.
  - SQLite accepts single-quoted names, and the roadmap's restrictions don't
  mention them.

  **Open for Markus:** whether single-quoted names are rejected; what "any
  integer value" includes (sign, hex, separators); what an error promises to
  match; whether SQLite may classify rejected statements in production; whether
  the own API is public.

  **Not verified:** meyer's coverage claims; whether musql's harness runs in CI;
  whether Turso compares `EXPLAIN` automatically; the LR argument that default
  reductions never move the error token; whether SQLite has its own "not
  supported" messages; the authorizer as a way to see dequoted names; whether
  modernc's newest tag has `NewConnector`.

⏺ Pull request #18 failed CI on one flaky test, which I fixed and pushed; CI is
  running again.

  The test was TestClient_connections in s3store, and the fault was in the test,
  not the client. It makes the content fail after its last byte and expected an
  "unknown outcome" every time. The store can answer before that late read
  fails, and then the create simply succeeds. Both are legal, so the test now
  accepts both and still requires that the error never looks like a refusal. It
  passed 100 runs plain and 50 under the race detector.

  Still running: the late-write builder and the tokenizer builder.

› Message from fabrik:builder
  The tokenizer is built and committed on branch
  `worktree-agent-a081df3f8405340b0`, in two commits on `560cc9a` (`477201a`,
  `09cb1fe`). It is not pushed. On every check it matches SQLite's
  `sqlite3GetToken` token for token: the hand-written cases, every keyword, the
  exhaustive short inputs, and 271.6 million fuzz inputs, with no difference
  found. `make test`, `make lint` (0 issues in all three modules, simulation
  lint and Buf quiet) and `make test-asan FUZZTIME=60s` all pass.

  **Package and API** (`internal/sql/token`, in `/Users/maragubot/Developer/s4ql
  /.claude/worktrees/agent-a081df3f8405340b0/internal/sql/token/`)
  - `Kind`: one kind per TK_ code the tokenizer can give, 33 plain kinds and 136
  keyword kinds for the 147 keywords. Some keywords share a code as in SQLite:
  `LikeKw`, `JoinKw`, `CTimeKw`, `Temp`. It has `String()`, which gives the TK_
  name, and `IsKeyword()`.
  - `Next(z string) (Kind, int)`: a case-by-case port of `sqlite3GetToken`. It
  copies SQLite's `aiClass` and `sqlite3CtypeMap` tables as they are, and reads
  any byte past the end as NUL.
  - `Tokens(src string) []Token`, with `Token{Kind, Offset, Len}` and
  `Token.Text(src)`.
  - At the end of the text, or at a NUL byte, `Next` gives `(Illegal, 0)`
  exactly as SQLite does, and `Tokens` stops there. I chose this over a separate
  end kind so that kinds map one to one onto TK_ codes. The parser can spot a
  NUL by comparing where the tokens stop with `len(src)`.
  - The package comment says what it promises and what it leaves to the parser,
  with line numbers in the vendored `sqlite3.c`.

  **How the oracle is reached, and why**
  - The oracle is a thin wrapper in the binding's own translation unit, not a
  test-only package with its own copy of SQLite. In `internal/sqlite/sqlite.go`,
  a static C function `s4ql_tokenize` sits in the preamble, and two new Go
  functions use it: `sqlite.Tokenize(src) []sqlite.Token{Type, Len}` and
  `sqlite.Keywords()`.
  - A separate package would include the amalgamation from another directory,
  and the go command does not track files there. That is the stale-build bug the
  binding's review found and fixed with `source.h`; the wrapper inherits that
  fix.
  - This way there is no second compile of SQLite, normally or under `-asan`.
  The oracle is also the same object the cache runs, so the two cannot drift.
  - The cost is two exported functions in the production binding that production
  does not call.
  - The tests never hard-code TK_ numbers. They read the `#define TK_` lines
  from the vendored `sqlite3.c`, where each appears twice, and fail if the two
  copies disagree. `TestKind` also checks that every code `sqlite3GetToken` can
  assign (read from its body and from the `aKWCode` table) has exactly one Go
  kind, and that no Go kind is left over.

  **Tests and fuzzing**
  - 175 hand-written cases, each written out by hand and also compared with the
  oracle.
  - Every input of 1 and 2 bytes, every 3-byte input over a 40-byte alphabet,
  and every 4-byte input over a 19-byte alphabet.
  - Every keyword in lower, upper and mixed case, plus eight near-keywords per
  keyword.
  - `FuzzTokens` is added to the `Makefile` fuzz loop, so `make fuzz` and `make
  test-asan` run it.
  - Native fuzzing: 30 minutes with `-parallel 8`, 271,578,460 executions, 580
  corpus entries, no difference from SQLite.
  - Under ASan the fuzzer ran 60 seconds: 1,042,797 executions, clean.
  - Because no fuzz input ever differed, no new hand-written cases came from
  fuzzing. One hand-written expectation of mine was wrong (`1_e5` is one
  `QNUMBER`); the oracle caught it.
  - Four deliberate breakages each made several tests fail, and each was
  restored: the vertical-tab rule, the `/*` at end of input, the `n > 0`
  condition on Tcl suffixes, and `WINDOW` removed from the keyword table.
  - The 4-byte test first used the 40-byte alphabet and took about 32 s per run
  under `-race`, so I cut that alphabet to 19 bytes. The package now takes under
  10 s with `-race` and 5.6 s under `-asan`.

  **Left to the parser** (listed in the package comment)
  - Skipping spaces, and skipping comments only while
  `SQLITE_DBCONFIG_ENABLE_COMMENTS` is on.
  - The `unrecognized token` error, and stopping at the first illegal token.
  - The digit-separator check in `sqlite3DequoteNumber`.
  - Deciding whether `WINDOW`, `OVER` and `FILTER` are keywords from the tokens
  around them.
  - The synthetic `;` and end token fed at end of input.
  - The `SQLITE_LIMIT_SQL_LENGTH` and interrupt checks.
  - All token values: unquoting, number conversion and range, blob bytes, and
  keywords read as names.

  **Research note corrections** (`docs/research/sql-front-end.md` §1.1 and §1.3)
  - `/*` as the last two bytes of input is not a comment but `/` then `*`.
  `select 1 /*` fails with `near "*": syntax error`, while `select 1 /* `
  returns 1.
  - Tcl suffixes (`::` and `(…)`) work after all four named variable forms, not
  only `$`. `select :a(b)`, `select #a(b)` and `select @a::b` each return a row.
  - A malformed blob is illegal up to and including the next quote.
  - A NUL byte inside a token ends that token.
  - §1.3 now says how the oracle was built.
  - I also added the wrapper to `internal/sqlite/README.md`.

  **Self-review**: the code-review skill found no correctness bugs. I fixed:
  - `sqlite.Tokenize` now panics on texts of 2 GiB or more instead of
  overflowing a C int.
  - Token lengths are passed as `sqlite3_int64` on both sides.
  - The `Tokens` doc now says it carries on past an illegal token, where
  SQLite's parser stops.
  - The tests read `sqlite3.c` once instead of up to three times.
  - `todo.md` held built work; it now holds only what is open.

  I left two findings as they are. The byte reader closure is inlined at every
  use (checked with `-gcflags=-m`). The TK_ parsing appears in two test
  packages; sharing it would need an export in the production binding.

  **Decisions for you**
  - `docs/todo.md` has a new item, "The SQL front end", with one open question:
  should the engine refuse SQL containing a NUL byte, as `Conn.Prepare` does, or
  read up to the NUL as SQLite does?
  - Whether two exported test-oriented functions in `internal/sqlite` are
  acceptable to you (Markus too, if you think it needs him).
  - The `README.md` Status section is not updated because the brief said not to
  touch the README. It needs a line on merge.

  The diary is `docs/diary/2026-11-13-tokenizer.md`, three steps.

  Ran 1 shell command (ctrl+o to expand)

  ## Report: workload for writes that land late

  Branch: `worktree-agent-a8569e9b46c707813`. It has two commits on top of
  `main` (`186ccdb`): `6c97e52` changes `simstore`, and `2bc636e` adds the
  workload, the planted bug, and the docs. Nothing is pushed.

  **No fault was found in the core or in the harness.** Over 1000 seeds of the
  new workload, each run twice, no seed violated an invariant and no pair of
  traces differed.

  ### What I built

  **`simstore`** (`internal/store/simstore/`):
  - Two new fields on `Faults`: `Long` and `LongBy`. A write that `Late` or
  `AfterCrash` holds back now waits up to `LongBy` with probability `Long`, and
  up to `LateBy` otherwise.
  - With `Long` at zero no random number is drawn, so `AllFaults` and every
  existing workload behave exactly as before. I checked this: the hashes of all
  traces of `single`, `full` and `hammer` in `TestSimulation` are identical on
  this branch and on `main`.
  - A new method, `Store.Landings`, records every write that was held back when
  it arrives: when it was sent, when it landed, and whether it was applied or
  refused.
  - To keep the merge with the other builder easy, the new code is in new files
  (`late.go`, `late_test.go`). `client.go` gains a few lines, and `simstore.go`
  only gains the two fields and the slice of landings.

  **`core` tests**:
  - **`lateWrites(change)`** in `workloads_test.go`:
    - three instances, two crashes with restarts, closes, and two-minute
  cut-offs;
    - `Late` and `AfterCrash` at 0.8, and `Long` 0.5 with `LongBy` 15 minutes;
    - callers that run for about 15 simulated minutes, at about 8000 steps per
  seed.
  - **`TestSimulation`** runs it for 10 seeds, each twice, and compares the
  traces. It also asserts that:
    - both kinds of write (manifest swap, commit-object create) land after
  another node took the lease in at least a quarter of the seeds;
    - in some seed, each kind lands after other nodes took the lease twice,
  after another node committed, and later than `LateBy` allows.
  - **`run.verifyLandings`** in `simulation_test.go` counts what became of every
  held write against the manifest history. It is also a new check: a late
  manifest swap that is applied after the lease changed hands must be a take of
  a free lease, otherwise it is a violation.
    - To confirm the check fires, I briefly made the store skip the version
  check for swaps older than 70 s. The first five seeds all found violations,
  two of them through this check. The change was undone.
  - `liveness()` now includes `LongBy` in its bound when writes can be held
  back. `S4QL_SIM_WORKLOAD=late` runs the workload in `TestSimulationSeed`.

  ### The planted bug the workload finds

  None of the existing flight or salt bugs fit:
  - Over 100 seeds the workload found `settleByRead`, `lostMeansFailed` and
  `keepSalt` in none.
  - It does find `lateVersion` (100), `ackUnknown` (66), `ownDuration` (65),
  `takeoverAfterRelease` (60), `serveUnknown` (34) and `renewOutsideLock` (10),
  but none of those needs a long delay.

  So I planted a new bug, **`resendTake`**: a take of the lease that got no
  answer is sent again with the same bytes, as a retrying client would. This
  breaks the condition the core README states under "A take that arrives late":
  nothing sends a take twice with the same bytes.
  - The manifest without a lease entry is the only one whose version comes back.
  If the first attempt of the take lands after the next release at the same
  head, the manifest gets back a version it had before.
  - **Its workload** is a variant of `lateWrites`: 2 instances, callers that
  only read, 30 closes, `LostRequest` 0.2, `Long` 1. With writes, the head moves
  and the free manifest at the old head never returns; `lateWrites` itself
  found the bug in 0 of 200 seeds.
  - **Its control** has no held-back writes.

  Seed counts, over 1000 seeds each:

  | Run | Seeds that found it |
  |---|---|
  | The bug's workload | 387 |
  | Same workload with `Long` off (holds of up to a minute) | 33 |
  | Control (no held-back writes) | 0 |
  | Same workload without the bug | 0 |

  The default run finds it four times within the first 10 seeds (2, 5, 6, 9).
  The test comment gives these numbers.

  ### Run times (`main` → this branch)

  The race and sanitizer binaries were run in turns with binaries built from
  `main`; the machine was under heavy load throughout.

  | Run | `main` | This branch | Load |
  |---|---|---|---|
  | `go test ./internal/core/` | 46 s | 45 s (measured 20 minutes apart) | 12–17
  |
  | `-race` | 88 s, 97 s | 104 s, 105 s | 15–17 |

  So about +10–15% under the race detector, and within noise otherwise. The
  1000-seed runs sit behind `S4QL_SIM_SEEDS`.

  ### Checks run

  - `make test`: passes.

※ recap: Tonight's goal is to move s4ql forward within 20% of weekly usage: research, tokenizer, and two storage fixes are merged, and one builder is still testing a failing file system. Next, I review and merge its branch.

› Message from fabrik:builder
  Three of the four decisions now have a test that fails when its mutant is
  applied: 1, 3, and 4. Decision 2 cannot be reached through any file system, so
  it is still uncovered. Writing the tests also turned up one fault in the
  core, which I fixed.

  Branch: `worktree-agent-a3fd95ac6eb146e8e`. It is
  `worktree-agent-a8569e9b46c707813` plus `origin/main`, merged at f10638e, then
  5 commits. Not pushed.

  ## Fault found in the core (fixed)
  Every catch-up event logged `commit.bytes` as 0. `replay` in
  `internal/core/replay.go` returned `(_ replayed, err error)` and set
  `done.bytes` in a deferred function. By then `return done, ...` had already
  copied the value, so the count never reached `DB.catchUp`. The fix is one
  line: the result is named `done`. `TestEvents` now checks the count against
  the size of the commit object; it failed with "99, got 0" before the fix.
  Commit 89401d5. I found it because the replay test now relies on that count
  (see below).

  ## The seams
  - **Spool file, in plain Go.** A new unexported field `Options.local` in
  `core.go` holds `spool *func(kind string, f *os.File) spoolFile`, which wraps
  each spool file ("commit" or "annotations"). `spool.f` is now a small
  interface (read, write, seek, close); `*os.File` satisfies it. Only
  `export_test.go` sets the field, through `Options.WrapSpools`. The field is a
  pointer so that `Options` stays comparable. Production pays one nil check per
  spool file. The test double, `spoolFaults` in `spool_test.go`, fails the next
  N reads with `syscall.EIO`.
  - **Cache, through SQLite.** I judged a shim VFS small and clean enough, so I
  built `internal/sqlite/faultvfs`: about 250 lines of C in a cgo preamble, plus
  a Go API.
    - It wraps SQLite's default VFS and replaces only `xOpen`. The file it wraps
  forwards all 18 methods.
    - It can fail open, read, write, truncate, sync, file size, and lock, chosen
  by kind of file (database, write-ahead log, journal, temporary file). A test
  arms one fault at a time: `Fault{Ops, Files, After, Times, Code}`.
    - It uses only the types of `sqlite3.h` and the function pointers SQLite
  hands out, so there is no second copy of SQLite and the symbol rules hold.
    - The binding gained `OpenVFS`, `FindVFS`, `RegisterVFS`, and
  `UnregisterVFS`; `Open` is now `OpenVFS(path, "")`. The core's `openConn`
  passes `Options.local.vfs`, which only `export_test.go` sets, through
  `UseVFS`.
    - A new depguard rule in `.golangci.yml` lets only test files import
  `faultvfs`; I checked that it fires on a non-test import. So no production
  build carries the shim.
    - Why not the alternatives: real failures differ between macOS, Linux, and
  the sanitizer container, and cannot be aimed at one operation. The unix VFS's
  `xSetSystemCall` is process-wide and depends on the platform.

  ## Which test kills which mutant
  I rebuilt the mutants from their names, because the old runner and lists were
  in another session's scratchpad. Each was applied with `go test -overlay`; a
  control without a change passes.
  - **`upload-spool-error-retried`** (`case content.err != nil && false:` in
  `Tx.upload`): killed by `TestDB_stop` / "should stop for a failure to read the
  spool file during the upload, and not try the upload again".
  - **`spool-length-error-ends`** (`if errors.Is(err, io.EOF) || err != nil {`
  after `ReadUvarint` in `spool.annotations`): killed by `TestDB_stop` / "should
  stop for a failure to read the length of an annotation from its spool file,
  and not commit without it".
  - **`replay-cache-error-as-broken`** (`(classify(err) != kindOther && false)
  || ...` after `apply`): killed by `TestDB_stop_cache` / "should stop for good
  with a local failure, and not a broken log, when the file system fails while
  replay applies a change, and keep the cache as it was". The mutant's stop says
  "the log is broken: sqlite: disk I/O error (code 778)".
    - How the test works: a commit object of 100 columns × 4000 rows is about
  twice SQLite's default 2 MB page cache, so replay writes pages to the
  write-ahead log before its commit. The fault (every write to the log fails) is
  armed before the instance opens.
    - The guard: the catch-up event's byte count must be below the size of the
  commit object. With too few rows the write would fail at the commit instead,
  and the test would pass without reaching the check; with 500 rows the guard
  now fails the test.

  Each test checks the error the caller sees (`ErrStopped` and `ErrLocal`, not
  `ErrOutcomeUnknown`, and not `ErrLogBroken` for replay), that the instance
  stopped, that the log head is unchanged, that the lease is released, and that
  the instance opened again serves the old state, or for replay catches up from
  an untouched cache.

  ## Still unreachable, and what it would take
  - **`read-end-swallows-local` (decision 2).** No file system can make it fail.
  SQLite 3.54.1 ends a read transaction through functions that return nothing
  (`sqlite3RollbackAll`, `pager_unlock`, `sqlite3WalEndReadTransaction`). So a
  `ROLLBACK` after only reads returns `SQLITE_OK`, or a "no transaction is
  active" error, which is not a local failure. Both reviewers confirmed this
  against the source. With `err = nil` always in `ReadTx.end`, the whole package
  passes. A faultvfs test shows the rollback meets no operation that can fail.
  Only out of memory, in the prepare of the `ROLLBACK`, could still reach the
  check.
  - The other two remain as before: the order in which connections open (needs
  real parallelism after a crash) and the sync of the cache (needs a power
  loss).

  The core README list and the todo bullet now say this.

  ## Run time of `go test -count=1 ./internal/core/`
  - Plain: 46.0 s before, 45.7 s after.
  - Under `-race`: 114 s without the new tests, 112 s with them — the same
  within noise.
  - The replay test alone takes about 0.6 s, about 9 s under `-race`, and about
  7 s under asan.

  ## Checks
  - `make lint`: 0 issues. `make test`: passes.
  - `make test-asan`: passes, but I ran it with `FUZZTIME=5s` instead of CI's 30
  s; no fuzzed code changed.
  - `-count=50`, plain and under `-race`: passes. The first race run caught a
  race in my own test: 15 of 50 runs read the catch-up event before the lease
  task had logged it. The check now comes after `DB.Close`, which waits for that
  task, and it then passed 50 of 50 under `-race` with a plain run alongside
  for load.

  ## Self-review
  Two reviewers, both of whom confirmed the kills and the decision-2 claim. What
  I fixed from them:
  - `VFS.Close` freed the VFS under a connection that has no file open, such as
  one to `:memory:` (a use after free under asan). Its doc now states the
  contract, and the open-file check stays as a safety net.
  - Any method after `Close` crashed the test binary in C. Now a second `Close`
  does nothing and the others panic in Go.
  - The replay test could pass without reaching its decision. Fixed by the
  byte-count guard, and it now arms its fault before the instance starts, as
  AGENTS.md asks.
  - Four fault kinds and `After` were untested. They now have tests, and six
  mutants of the C that passed before are now killed.
  - A file with no shared memory would have been offered `xShmMap`, and a failed
  open could leave the count of open files too high. Both fixed.
  - The spool double embedded `*os.File`, which let some of its methods go
  around the faults. It now holds the file in a field.
  - Smaller fixes: `Options` stays comparable, a comment that was wrong on
  macOS, the README wording, `FindVFS` and a NUL byte, `VFSName` removed,
  constants used once inlined.

  One thing I left as it is: the comments in `core.go` and `spool.go` that say
  only the package's tests set `local` follow the existing `bugs` comment.

  ## For you to decide

  1. The parser. I recommend our own parser for all of SQLite's grammar, with a
     pass after it that rejects what the roadmap does not list. Only that can
     tell a SQLite syntax error from "not supported". A new Go project,
     sqlc-dev/meyer, does this and would serve as a reference; it is too young
     to depend on.
  2. What an error promises to match. I recommend the result code, the extended
     code, and the message text for statements of a supported shape. The codes
     alone cannot tell no such table from table already exists.
  3. How "not supported" is marked. I recommend a sentinel error and a message
     that cannot be SQLite's.

     the driver cannot carry the stop of an instance or a context on Close and
     Commit. Whether it is public is your call.

     includes, a NUL byte in SQL text, and five SQLite errors the roadmap does
     not list.

  First decision: the parser

  The question is whether the parser knows only the grammar of the slices, or
  all of SQLite's grammar with a pass after it that rejects what the roadmap
  does not list.

  I recommend the full grammar, written by us. Only a parser that knows all of
  SQLite's grammar can tell "SQLite would call this a syntax error" from "valid
  SQLite that s4ql does not support", and give SQLite's own near "X": syntax 
  error for the first. A slice-only parser stops earlier than SQLite does, so
  its error for anything outside the subset is its own invention.

  The cost is a large parser surface that slice 1 never executes but that must
  still be right, because its job is to classify. It can be checked against
  SQLite's own acceptance over SQLite's test scripts and by fuzzing, as the
  tokenizer was.

  A new Go project, sqlc-dev/meyer, is a hand-written port of all of SQLite's
  grammar that claims exact syntax messages. It has one author and seven
  commits, so I would use it as a reference to compare against and not as a
  dependency.

  Full grammar with a reject pass, written by us?

✻ Cooked for 13s · done Monday 9:56 AM

❯ Yes, that sounds reasonable. But write your own parser instead of relying on  
  other people's work. Should be doable with SQLite as the oracle? Are we using 
  just SQLite's parser for that, or do we need to go through the entire SQLite  
  engine?                                                                       

⏺ Noted: our own parser, no code from other projects. I'll keep meyer out
  entirely, also as a starting point.

  On the oracle: we have to go through more than SQLite's parser, but not
  through the whole engine. SQLite has no parser-only entry point. Its grammar
  actions call the code generator directly, and it builds no syntax tree that it
  hands out. The way in is sqlite3_prepare_v2, which runs the tokenizer, the
  parser, and the code generator, and stops before anything executes.

  That gives the oracle three properties:

  - Accept or reject, and the syntax message, come from SQLite itself. A
    statement that fails with near "X": syntax error, unrecognized token, or
    incomplete input failed in the parser. One that prepares, or fails with
    something like no such table, got through the parser.
  - A semantic error can hide a later syntax error. The research measured this:
    table t1 already exists is raised in the middle of parsing and wins over a
    fault further on. So for pure syntax checks we prepare against a schema
    where names resolve, or an empty one, and treat the remaining cases as
    "SQLite does not say".
  - The tree has no oracle. SQLite can tell us whether a statement parses, not
    what our tree should look like. The tree is checked indirectly: the engine
    runs the statement and the rows and errors are compared with SQLite's.

  I have not checked whether the private sqlite3RunParser could be called
  directly, as we did with sqlite3GetToken. It would still run the code
  generator, so I expect it to add nothing over prepare.

  Shall I go on to the next decision, what an error promises to match?

  What it measures, with our SQLite binding as the oracle:

  - Acceptance: over a large corpus, what rqlite/sql accepts that SQLite rejects
    (the dangerous kind for "reject, never reinterpret"), and the reverse.
  - Tokens: its lexer against SQLite's own tokenizer, and whether our tokenizer
    could feed its parser.
  - Errors: whether it stops at the token SQLite names in near "X", so that
    SQLite's syntax message could be produced with it.
  - The tree: whether it keeps names exactly as written, with their quotes, and
    every spelling the reject pass must see (int against integer, strict, if 
    exists).
  - The project: licence, maintenance, tests, dependencies, and robustness under
    fuzzing.

  that note read rqlite/sql from source only and said its errors and keyword

  checks that by measurement.

❯ sqlc-dev/meyer notes that the 21k cases are extracted from the SQLite test

⏺ Yes, the part meyer uses is public. SQLite's testing page says of its TCL
  tests: "They are contained in the same source tree as the SQLite core and like
  the SQLite core are in the public domain." It gives 1390 files with 51445
  distinct test cases.

  What we can and cannot use, per that page (https://sqlite.org/testing.html):

  - The TCL tests: public domain, in the test/ directory of SQLite's source
    tree. We don't have them in the repository today, because we vendor only the
    amalgamation.
  - The fuzz corpora: historical cases from AFL, OSS Fuzz, and dbsqlfuzz are
    kept as database files in the same source tree, so they are public too.
  - TH3: proprietary, so not available to us.
  - dbsqlfuzz: proprietary as well; only its past findings are in the tree.

  One catch with the TCL tests: they are TCL scripts, not lists of SQL. The SQL
  sits inside commands like do_execsql_test, often with TCL variables
  substituted in, so pulling statements out is imperfect. Meyer's 21k cases
  against the page's 51k test cases suggests it extracted what could be
  extracted cleanly, though I'm inferring that from the two numbers.

  For us the expected results don't need extracting at all. We only need the SQL
  text, and our pinned SQLite gives the expected answer for each statement, as
  it did for the tokenizer. The meyer agent is already told to extract
  statements from the TCL scripts for its corpus, so we'll see how well that
  works.

✻ Cogitated for 34s · done Monday 10:09 AM

❯ I like the conformance section in their readme, mirrors our approach to an    
  extent: https://github.com/sqlc-dev/meyer#conformance                         

⏺ Fetch(https://github.com/sqlc-dev/meyer)
  ⎿  Received 248.5KB (200 OK)

⏺ I read it, and I agree: it is the same idea as ours, with SQLite as the judge
  and the project's own expectations ranked below it. Its README even states the
  limit I described earlier: "There is no upstream parse-tree oracle — SQLite
  cannot dump its parse tree."

  It has two things our plan lacked, and both are methods we can adopt without
  taking any of their code:

  - A round-trip property for the tree. Each accepted statement is rendered back
    to SQL and parsed again, and the two trees are compared. That catches a
    dropped clause or a wrongly grouped operator, which accept-or-reject cannot
    see. It is a direct answer to the tree having no oracle.
  - One-token mutations over SQLite's fuzz corpora. Its difftest runs about 36k
    inputs from SQLite's fuzzdata databases against a live SQLite, each as is
    and then mutated one token at a time, comparing message and byte offset.

  Two differences from our side:

  - We get a check they cannot have. Our engine executes what it parses, so rows
    and errors are compared with SQLite's. A tree that is wrong in meaning but
    survives the round trip still shows up there.
  - We don't compare byte offsets yet. Our binding does not expose
    sqlite3_error_offset. If we want SQLite's offsets, that is a small addition
    to the binding.

  The meyer research agent is checking these claims by running them, including
  where the 21k cases come from and whether the tool really compares what the
  README says. Both agents are still running.

✻ Brewed for 19s · done Monday 10:10 AM

› Message from general-purpose
  ## rqlite/sql as s4ql's parser: research note committed

  **Branch:** `worktree-agent-a5035f23b6f597177`, commit `0dafc1d` ("Add
  research note on rqlite/sql as the SQL parser"). It holds only
  `docs/research/rqlite-sql.md` and one new entry in `docs/research/README.md`.
  Nothing is pushed, and there is no pull request. The secrets check is clean.
  `go.mod`, `go.sum` and `internal/sqlite/sqlite.go` are back to their committed
  state, and the temporary package is deleted. There was no contact with the
  rqlite/sql project; I only read it through clone and the read-only GitHub API.

  **Short answer:** rqlite/sql parses 96.8% of what SQLite parses. But it also
  accepts some of what SQLite rejects, and some of that changes meaning. Its
  tree drops distinctions the "supported?" pass needs. Its errors are not
  SQLite's. The evidence favours the parser we already agreed to write, with
  rqlite/sql as a reference and a source of test inputs, not as a dependency.
  The note says what the evidence favours; it does not decide.

  **State of the project**
  - MIT licence. Clone is at commit `5c0832f` (2026-10-16). 233 commits, nearly
  all by one maintainer, Philip O'Toole; 83 commits in October 2026, several
  pull requests titled "fixes by Claude".
  - No tags or releases. No open issues or pull requests.
  - Its tests are hand-written expectations with 76.4% coverage. It has no fuzz
  test, and no test compares it with SQLite.
  - `benbjohnson/sql` is a GitHub fork of rqlite/sql, frozen at 2024-03-09.
  rqlite/sql is 187 commits ahead of it.
  - Brad Fitzpatrick has one commit and four issues.
  - rqlite itself parses only statements it wants to rewrite (`random()`, time
  functions, `RETURNING`, `EXPLAIN`). It sends anything that fails to parse to
  SQLite unchanged and recovers from parser panics, so it barely depends on
  correct rejection.

  **Corpus:** 68,032 distinct statements:

  | Source | Statements |
  |---|---|
  | SQLite 3.54.1's TCL test scripts | 47,418 |
  | SQLite's documentation | 1,178 |
  | rqlite/sql's own tests | 726 |
  | meyer's corpus (new ones only) | 95 |
  | `internal/sql/token` tests | 325 |
  | Generated variations | 18,290 |

  After leaving out 358 statements whose parse SQLite stopped early (a check
  inside the grammar failed before the end) and 18 multi-statement texts, 67,656
  count.

  **Measured results**

  *Acceptance (point 2)*

  | SQLite says | rqlite/sql accepts | rqlite/sql rejects | rqlite/sql panics |
  |---|---|---|---|
  | parses (53,965) | 52,217 | 1,748 (3.2%) | 0 |
  | syntax error (13,691) | 154 (1.1%) | 13,534 | 3 |

  On SQLite's own test statements alone, rqlite/sql accepts 47 of 371 syntax
  errors (12.7%).

  A second check compared SQLite's `EXPLAIN` of each accepted statement with the
  `EXPLAIN` of rqlite/sql's `String()` reprint, over 18,286 statements. 67
  differ:
  - 42 come from changed quoting in `ALTER TABLE`, which SQLite stores as
  written.
  - 10 are digit separators read as an alias.
  - 8 are `LIMIT a, b`, where the meaning is reversed.
  - 7 reprints do not prepare at all.

  *Tokenizer (point 3)*
  - The tokenizer is built into the parser and cannot be swapped without a fork,
  so `internal/sql/token` cannot feed it.
  - Token boundaries match SQLite's in 99.63% of statements. Boundaries and
  token kinds both match in 83.3%; the gap is words that one side reads as a
  keyword and the other as a name.
  - Positions are counted in runes, not bytes. The source documents `Column` as
  a byte count.
  - `ROWID`, `STORED`, `STRICT`, `TRUE` and `FALSE` are keywords in rqlite/sql
  but names in SQLite.

  *Errors (point 4)*
  - Errors are Go-style, e.g. `1:8: expected expression, found 'EOF'`. The error
  carries only a position and a message, not the failing token.

  | SQLite's message | Cases | rqlite/sql stops at SQLite's token |
  |---|---|---|
  | `near "X"` | 12,801 | 11,942 (93.3%) |
  | `unrecognized token` | 278 | 245 (88.1%) |
  | `incomplete input` | 451 | 439 say EOF (97.3%) |

  Many of the misses report the position of the statement's first keyword
  instead (e.g. `drop - t1` reports position 1:1).

  **Most important disagreements**
  - **Accepted by rqlite/sql, rejected by SQLite (the dangerous kind):**
    - `DELETE ... ORDER BY/LIMIT`: 45
    - `select select 1` (SELECT without parentheses as an expression): 23
    - `update t x set ...` (alias without `AS`): 21
    - join keywords as type names, e.g. `a left`: 19
    - `NULL` as a name, e.g. `drop table null`: 17
    - a number followed by letters, e.g. `select 1x` read as `1 AS x`: 16
  - **Accepted by both, different meaning:**
    - `select 1_000` is read as `1 AS "_000"`.
    - `v1 int null` becomes a column whose type is `int null`, with no NULL
  constraint.
    - `select true from t` is always the literal TRUE; SQLite returns the column
  `"true"` if one exists.
    - `LIMIT 1, 2`: the field named `LimitExpr` holds what SQLite treats as the
  offset, and `String()` prints `LIMIT 1 OFFSET 2`.
  - **Rejected by rqlite/sql, accepted by SQLite:**
    - fallback keywords as table names or aliases: 881, including `SELECT * FROM
  temp.sqlite_master`
    - `ROWID`/`STRICT`/`TRUE`/`FALSE` as names: 196
    - single-quoted names: 102
    - `$::name` parameters: 100
    - `ALTER TABLE ... CONSTRAINT`: 53
    - virtual table arguments: 47
  - **For slice 1:** `NULL` as a name is accepted; `rowid`, `strict`, `true` and
  `false` as column names are rejected; brackets and backticks cannot be told
  from double quotes in the tree; a repeated `strict` collapses into one; error
  positions miss SQLite's in about 7% of cases.

  **What the tree keeps and loses**
  - **Keeps:** positions of keywords and names, `IF EXISTS`, `STRICT`, `WITHOUT
  ROWID`, `int` versus `integer`, numbers and parameters as written.
  - **Loses:**
    - the quote style: `"x"`, `[x]`, `` `x` `` and an alias `'x'` all become
  `Quoted: true`
    - raw name text and end positions
    - some spellings: `<>`/`!=`, `TEMP`/`TEMPORARY`, `0X`/`0x`
    - a repeated `strict`
    - spacing inside multi-word type names
  - A rejection pass needs the source text next to the tree. It cannot see what
  the parser already reinterpreted, such as digit separators.

  **Robustness (point 7)**
  - The depth limit of 1000 prevents stack overflow.
  - 300 s of fuzzing (28.8M runs) found one logged panic site. The site of about
  50k panics on non-UTF-8 input was not logged.
  - Compound selects parse in quadratic time: 7.7 s for 30,000 `UNION`s.

  **Bugs that could be reported upstream** (listed in §9 of the note; nothing
  sent):
  - **Panic:** `create table zz (a, check (1), b)` panics with `assert failed`
  (parser.go line 536); SQLite says `near "b"`.
  - **`LIMIT a, b`:** `String()` reverses its meaning, and rqlite itself
  rewrites statements with `String()`.
  - **Wrongly accepted:**
    - digit separators and a number followed by letters, read as aliases
    - `NULL` as a name
    - bare `SELECT` as an operand
    - alias without `AS` on UPDATE/DELETE
    - join keywords and `NULL` as type-name words
    - `t.*` inside an expression
    - `/*` as the last two bytes
    - U+00A0 read as whitespace
    - `INSERT ... SELECT ... ON CONFLICT` without `WHERE`
  - **Wrongly rejected:** `temp.sqlite_master`, `rowid`/`true` as column names,
  a leading byte order mark, `$::a`.
  - **Other:** quadratic `UNION` parsing; the `Pos.Column` documentation says
  bytes but it counts runes.

  **Not verified**
  - How the 83 GitHub repositories that list rqlite/sql in `go.mod` use it.
  - The panic site for the fuzz inputs that were not valid UTF-8.
  - When SQLite added `ALTER TABLE ... CONSTRAINT`.
  - That every extracted TCL brace block is SQL the tests really run; a few are
  templates.

  **Scripts and data (not committed)**, all under
  `/private/tmp/claude-502/-Users-maragubot-Developer-s4ql--claude-worktrees-obj
  ect-store/f1343515-adca-4ca0-9eb0-f507768f8fc7/scratchpad/`:
  - `scripts/zzrqlite/`: the Go test harness (corpus extraction, comparison,
  `EXPLAIN` check, tree dumps, keyword sets, nesting probes, fuzz target, bug
  probes)
  - `scripts/binding-probe.patch`: the temporary binding functions
  - `scripts/go.mod.orig`, `scripts/go.sum.orig`
  - `results/`: `corpus.jsonl`, `results.jsonl`, `meaning.jsonl`,
  `classify.txt`, `panics.jsonl`
  - `rqlite-src/sql` and `benbjohnson-src/sql`: the clones
  - `corpus/`: SQLite source tarball, SQLite docs, meyer clone

⏺ Agent "Research rqlite/sql as dependency" finished · 34m 40s

⏺ The rqlite/sql research is in, and the measurements speak against using it as
  our parser. The note is on main as docs/research/rqlite-sql.md. The meyer
  research is still running, so I'll hold the recommendation until both are in.

  Measured over 67,656 statements, 47,418 of them extracted from SQLite's own
  TCL tests, with our pinned SQLite as the judge:

  ┌───────────────────────┬────────────────────┬──────────────┬────────┐
  │      SQLite says      │ rqlite/sql accepts │   rejects    │ panics │
  ├───────────────────────┼────────────────────┼──────────────┼────────┤
  │ parses (53,965)       │ 52,217             │ 1,748 (3.2%) │ 0      │
  ├───────────────────────┼────────────────────┼──────────────┼────────┤
  │ syntax error (13,691) │ 154 (1.1%)         │ 13,534       │ 3      │
  └───────────────────────┴────────────────────┴──────────────┴────────┘

  The problems, in order of weight for us:

  - It changes meaning in places. select 1_000 is read as 1 AS "_000". LIMIT 1, 
    2 is printed back with offset and limit swapped. v1 int null becomes a
    column of type int null with no constraint.
  - It accepts some of what SQLite rejects. Examples are drop table null, select
    1x (read as 1 AS x), and update t x set .... This is the dangerous kind for
    "reject, never reinterpret".
  - Its tree loses what our reject pass needs. "x", [x], and `x` all become one
    "quoted" flag, raw name text is gone, and a repeated strict collapses into
    one.
  - Its errors are not SQLite's. They are Go-style, and it stops at the token
    SQLite names in about 93% of the syntax errors.
  - Its tokenizer is built in, so ours cannot feed it without a fork.

  On the project itself:

  - Maintainer: nearly all 233 commits are by rqlite's maintainer, Philip
    O'Toole, with 83 of them in October 2026. Ben Johnson's repository is now a
    frozen fork of it, and Brad Fitzpatrick has one commit.
  - Tests: it has no fuzz test and no test that compares it with SQLite.
  - Use in rqlite: rqlite parses only the statements it wants to rewrite and
    passes everything else to SQLite unchanged, so it barely depends on correct
    rejection.

  The agent found about fifteen bugs that could be reported upstream, among them
  a panic on create table zz (a, check (1), b). They are listed in section 9 of
  the note, and nothing was sent.

✻ Churned for 1s · done Monday 10:43 AM

❯ Maybe silly idea, but: would it be possible to read the SQLite parser's       
  output out from its memory?                                                   

⏺ Agent(Research reading SQLite's parse tree) Opus 5.5
  ⎿  Backgrounded agent (↓ to manage · ctrl+o to expand)

⏺ Not silly, and I think it is possible, but none of what follows is verified
  yet. I've started a third Opus research agent to check it by doing it.

  The reason it could work is the same as for the tokenizer: we compile SQLite
  ourselves, so C code in our binding can reach SQLite's private functions and
  structs.

  What I expect, from memory:

  - SQLite may have its own tree printer. Its source has a treeview.c that
    prints select trees in debug builds. If that holds for 3.54.1, meyer's
    "SQLite cannot dump its parse tree" is only true of the public API.
  - We could walk the structs ourselves. The hard part is catching the tree
    before the code generator consumes and frees it. Possible ways in: the
    parse-only mode that ALTER TABLE ... RENAME uses, or reading what SQLite
    keeps in its schema. A view stores the tree of its select, and a trigger
    stores the trees of the statements in its body.
  - It is not a syntax tree. By the time a tree exists, the parser has already
    changed things: != and <> share one code, and names are dequoted. So the
    check would be "our tree, lowered by the same rules, equals SQLite's". That
    would still catch wrong operator grouping or a clause attached to the wrong
    select, which accept-or-reject cannot see.
  - Some statements have no tree at all, because the parser generates code for
    them directly.

  The agent is to produce real dumps for a handful of statements, a table of
  which statement kinds are reachable by which route, and the cost of each route
  (a debug build differs from the production one; private structs change
  between versions).

  Running now: this agent and the meyer research.

› Message from general-purpose
  **Branch:** `worktree-agent-afeb6e898afb83f6a`, commit `4872928`. It holds
  `docs/research/meyer.md` and one new entry in `docs/research/README.md`. Not
  pushed. The sibling writing `rqlite-sql.md` may also add a README entry, so
  the index can conflict on merge.

  **Short answer.** On acceptance and error messages, meyer is very close to
  SQLite and far ahead of rqlite/sql. My checks found faults that meyer's own
  tests cannot see, and one of them changes what an accepted `where` clause
  means.

  **Corpus.** 535,220 statements, measured against SQLite 3.54.1 through the
  binding. 125,507 are independent of meyer's corpus:
  - 63,979 from sqllogictest, the docs, rqlite/sql's tests, s4ql's token tests
  and quirks.
  - 41,998 from SQLite's fuzzdata, which meyer's difftest already uses.
  - 19,465 TCL statements that meyer's extractor did not take.
  - 65 from more than one of these.

  30,009 are in meyer's corpus, and 379,704 are one-token mutations. SQLite
  classified 526,495 of them; 7,475 stopped mid-parse and were left out.

  **Results:**

  | Point | meyer | rqlite/sql |
  |---|---|---|
  | 2. Agrees with SQLite on acceptance | 526,493 of 526,495 | 511,342 (97.12%)
  |
  | 2. Accepts what SQLite rejects | 2 | 4,626 |
  | 2. Rejects what SQLite accepts | 0 | 10,527 |
  | 4. Same message, where both reject | 304,706 of 304,706 | 0 of 300,082 |
  | 4. Same position | 304,685 (the 21 others are cases where SQLite gives -1) |
  244,229 (81.4%) |
  | 3. Tokens equal to sqlite3GetToken | 532,571 of 535,220; the other 2,649
  differ only by the WINDOW/OVER/FILTER decision, made in the lexer by design |
  not measured |

  - **Tokenizer:** meyer's lexer is built into the parser, so s4ql's tokenizer
  cannot feed it without changing meyer. s4ql's own tokenizer matched SQLite on
  all 535,220.
  - **Keyword-as-name templates** (16,491 statements): meyer agrees on 16,484,
  rqlite/sql on 13,259.
  - **Generated slice shapes** (111,841 statements, prepared on a schema where
  the names resolve): meyer agrees on all 102,828 that SQLite classified. 93 of
  64,290 messages differ, all from one cause. rqlite/sql gets 472 accept-wrong
  and 21,018 reject-wrong.

  **The most important disagreements:**
  1. **Wrong tree for `NOT x NOT NULL`.** I built an oracle for expression
  shape: SQLite evaluates the expression, and also evaluates meyer's tree
  printed with every operation in parentheses. Of 276,022 random expressions,
  450 gave different values, all from this one cause. meyer builds `(NOT x) NOT
  NULL`; SQLite builds `NOT (x NOT NULL)`. Measured on a table with one NULL
  row: `where not v1 not null` gives 1 row in SQLite and 0 under meyer's tree.
  The same cause makes meyer reject `'a' like not 'b' not null escape 'x'` (16
  inputs). meyer's round-trip test cannot catch it, because the renderer prints
  the tokens back in the same order. The cause is `parser/parse_expr.go` 103.
  2. **`select cast.* from t` is accepted** (also `raise`, `current_date`,
  `current_time`, `current_timestamp`). SQLite says `near ".": syntax error`.
  The cause is `parse_select.go` 169: it uses the `%fallback` keyword set in a
  state where the keyword has its own action.
  3. **Digit-separator error is not deferred.** For `select 1_ 2`, SQLite says
  `near "2"`; meyer says `unrecognized token: "1_"`. This one cause accounts for
  all 93 message differences in the slice shapes.
  4. **Smaller corners:**
     - `select 1__2__3`: SQLite reports `"12_2__3"`, meyer `"1__2__3"`.
     - Offsets: meyer gives the token offset where SQLite gives -1 (`#N` and
  digit separators).
     - Recursion: SQLite says `Recursion limit` from 2,494 nested parentheses;
  meyer accepts up to 9,998, on purpose.
     - Errors from grammar actions (`table t1 already exists` mid-statement,
  `unknown table option`, `LIMIT clause should come after UNION`) are outside
  meyer's scope by design.
  5. **rqlite/sql:**
     - It accepts `SELECT SELECT 1`; this pattern is 3,570 of its 4,626 wrong
  accepts.
     - It also accepts `DELETE … LIMIT`, `UPDATE t1 t1 SET`, `create table null
  (…)` and `pragma x = select`.
     - It rejects valid slice-1 names such as `'z'`, `true`, `rowid` and
  `strict`.
     - 35 `assert failed` panics, and it took more than 10 s on a compound
  select of 100,000 terms.

  **Robustness (point 7):**
  - meyer's own FuzzParse: 5 minutes, 24.7 million inputs, clean.
  - My differential fuzz of meyer against SQLite: two stops, both harness
  artifacts. One was an input ending in `/*`. The other was two action errors at
  one token, where SQLite's last message wins. After fixing those, 10 minutes
  and 87.3 million inputs, clean.
  - meyer had no panics or hangs on the corpus.

  **What the tree keeps and loses (points 5 and 6).**
  - Kept:
    - The raw text and quote style of every name: none, `"`, backtick, `[`, or
  `'`.
    - A byte span on every node.
    - Raw literals.
    - Type names as written (`int` and `integer` stay apart).
    - Table options in order with raw text, and the optional keywords.
    - A string used as a key column (`primary key ('a')`) stays a string
  literal, so it is visible.
  - Folded: `=`/`==`, `<>`/`!=`, and `TEMP`/`TEMPORARY`. The spans still hold
  the text.
  - So a pass after parsing can see and reject brackets, backticks,
  single-quoted names and unlisted clauses. It needs a reflection-based "every
  unallowed field is zero" check so that a field it forgot cannot slip through.
  The shape of the tree cannot be trusted without the evaluation oracle.

  **Comparison table:** section 9.2 of the note sets meyer, rqlite/sql and an
  own parser side by side on these properties with the numbers.

  **What the evidence favours (no decision taken):**
  - Neither depending on meyer as it is, nor rqlite/sql in any role except as a
  source of test inputs. rqlite/sql fails "reject, never reinterpret" outright.
  meyer reinterprets one `where` form, and as a dependency the fixes and the
  SQLite pin stay outside s4ql's control.
  - Between owning a copy of meyer and using it only as a reference, the
  evidence alone does not decide:
    - A copy starts from measured near-exact code with a few located faults.
    - Reference-only keeps meyer as an independent second implementation.
  Comparison is what found these bugs.
    - In either case, the trust rests on the harness: differential acceptance
  and messages, the slice generator, the mutator, the keyword templates and the
  evaluation oracle.

  **Project facts (point 1):**
  - MIT licence, one author (GitHub account `kyleconroy`), 7 commits and 6 PRs.
  - The parser was written in about 4 hours on 2026-07-30 in an agent loop
  (`CLAUDE.md`; PR 3 says "Runs 'the loop'"). Last commit 2026-08-25.
  - No dependencies, Go 1.24.
  - sqlc's main branch has used it since PR #4535 (2026-08-01), with an option
  for a grammar the default SQLite build lacks. No sqlc release uses it yet; the
  latest, v1.31.1, is older.
  - The 21,326 cases come from SQLite 3.53.4's TCL scripts, prepared on an empty
  in-memory database. Only 156 expect a rejection.
  - 3.53.4 to 3.54.1: `tokenize.c` and `mkkeywordhash.c` are identical.
  `parse.y` differs in 2 places, both action code: INDEXED BY carried through a
  parenthesized table, and the `IN` rewrite skipped with COLLATE. So meyer is

⏺ Agreed: our own parser, and no builder until we have gone through the test
  approaches and the last research agent has reported.

  On Nix, I'd hold the research until that agent is back, because its result
  decides whether there is anything to patch. It is checking several ways to get
  the tree without changing SQLite's source, and a patched copy only as the
  last resort. If one of the clean routes works, the Nix question goes away.

  If we do end up needing a patched SQLite for tests, my expectation is that Nix
  is more than the job needs. This is general knowledge of Nix, not checked
  against our setup:

  - What Nix is good at: fetching a source by hash, applying patches, and giving
    every machine the same toolchain.
  - What we already have: the amalgamation is vendored in internal/sqlite/c/,
    and internal/sqlite/update fetches and verifies it. A patch file in the
    repository, applied by that tool into a test-only copy, does the same job
    with no new tool.
  - Where Nix would not help: the Go command compiles cgo code from files in the
    package's directory. So the patched source has to end up as files in our
    tree either way, and Nix would only be another way of producing them.
  - What it would cost: every developer machine and CI would need Nix.

  Nix could still be worth a look for a different reason, a pinned toolchain for
  the whole project, but that is a separate question from the parser.

  So my proposal: when the parse-tree research lands, I bring you the test
  approaches for the parser one at a time, and if a patched SQLite is among
  them, we decide then whether Nix gets its own research. Is that order fine?

✻ Churned for 1s · done Monday 11:04 AM

❯ /model 
  ⎿  Kept model as Fable 5.2

› Message from general-purpose
  The answer to Markus's question is yes, for the statements that build a tree,
  and I committed the note on branch **`worktree-agent-ace8d188704080c06`**
  (commit `f9d8f78`, not pushed). It adds `docs/research/sqlite-parse-tree.md`
  and one entry in `docs/research/README.md`. The values of `AWS_ACCESS_KEY_ID`
  and `AWS_SECRET_ACCESS_KEY` from `.env` do not appear in either file.

  **Short answer.** `select`, `insert`, `update`, `delete` and `create
  table/index/view/trigger` build a tree. Every other statement's grammar action
  generates bytecode straight from tokens, so no tree of it ever exists. The
  meyer README claim ("SQLite cannot dump its parse tree") holds for the public
  API only. It is false for a project that compiles SQLite itself.

  **Routes that work, all run on 3.54.1:**
  1. **SQLite's tree printer (`treeview.c`).** It needs `-DSQLITE_DEBUG
  -DSQLITE_ENABLE_TREETRACE`; TREETRACE alone prints nothing. You switch it on
  with `.treetrace 0x10000` or `SQLITE_TESTCTRL_TRACEFLAGS`, and it prints to
  stdout through a process-wide global. It dumps at the entry of
  `sqlite3Select`, `sqlite3Insert`, `sqlite3Update` and `sqlite3Delete`. That is
  after the grammar's actions, before name resolution.
     - It loses quote style, every position, the `RETURNING` expressions (prints
  only the word) and `NATURAL`.
     - In normal mode, `x and 5 is null` (or `a in () and b`) replaces the whole
  `WHERE` with `0`.
     - Multi-row `VALUES` is compiled into a co-routine while parsing (since
  3.46.0). The first dump is the first row alone, and later rows appear in no
  dump.
     - Each statement also prints dumps of nested and rewritten statements, so
  "the first dump is the statement's tree" does not hold.
  2. **A C walker of our own over a rename-mode parse, in the production
  build.** The static `renameParseSql` (used by `ALTER TABLE ... RENAME`) is
  callable from the binding's translation unit. I wrap a `select` as `CREATE
  VIEW s4ql_v AS ...`, and an `insert`, `update` or `delete` as a trigger body.
  The trees stay in `pNewTable`, `pNewIndex` and `pNewTrigger`.
     - It also gives `create table/index/view/trigger`.
     - It keeps quote flags and source positions (rename tokens plus
  `Expr.w.iOfst`).
     - Rename mode skips the AND-false fold and turns multi-row `VALUES` into
  `UNION ALL`.
     - It works with `options.h` and `-DSQLITE_API=static`, and ran clean under
  ASan and UBSan over 167 statements.
     - The wrappers refuse with an error, never silently: parameters; another
  schema in a view; and in trigger bodies `WITH`, `RETURNING`, `DEFAULT VALUES`,
  `INDEXED BY` and schema-qualified targets. `CREATE TABLE ... AS SELECT` also
  fails.
     - The fixer reduces `main.t` to a flag.
  3. **Lemon's parser trace (`PRAGMA parser_trace`, debug build only).** It
  covers every statement kind, but as a derivation of rule texts, with token
  kinds instead of text. 14 of 412 unit rules are optimized out by Lemon (e.g.
  `expr ::= term`, `selectnowith ::= oneselect`).

  The schema after a statement ran also holds trees, but they are
  `EXPRDUP_REDUCE` copies without positions, so they give nothing route 2 lacks.
  The authorizer, `sqlite3_trace_v2`, the test controls, `declare_vtab` and
  `EXPLAIN` give no tree. A patched copy of `sqlite3.c` was analysed only, not
  built.

  **Coverage.**

  | Statement | Route 1 | Route 2 | Route 3 |
  |---|---|---|---|
  | `select` | yes | yes, as a view | yes |
  | `insert` / `update` / `delete` | yes | yes, as a trigger step, with the
  restrictions above | yes |
  | `create table/index/view/trigger` | no | yes | yes |
  | `create virtual table` | no | arguments as text only | yes |
  | `drop`, `alter`, transactions, `pragma`, `attach`/`detach`, `vacuum`,
  `analyze`, `reindex` | no | no | yes only |
  | `explain` | inner statement's tree | no | yes |

  **What a comparison could assert.** The tree is SQLite's internal form,
  already lowered by the grammar. Section 7 of the note lists about 30 rewrites
  with sources, for example:
  - `<>` and `!=` are one operator; `IN (5)` becomes `= +5`; `5 IS NULL` becomes
  `0`.
  - `LIKE` becomes `like(p, x)` with the operands swapped; `LIMIT a, b` is
  stored as `LIMIT b OFFSET a`.
  - `count(*)` equals `count()`, and `ALL` in a function is dropped.
  - Named windows are chained and their list is reversed.
  - `,`, `JOIN` and `INNER JOIN` are the same; `LEFT` and `LEFT OUTER` are the
  same.
  - `CHECK`, generated-column and index expressions are already resolved;
  defaults are wrapped in `SPAN`.

  So a check can assert that our tree, lowered by the same rules, equals
  SQLite's.
  - **It catches:** wrong grouping and associativity, clauses on the wrong term
  of a compound, dropped or swapped clauses that don't change results
  (`DISTINCT`, `NULLS FIRST`, `LIMIT` against `OFFSET`, frames, upsert details),
  name quoting and aliases, and name positions.
  - **It cannot catch:** anything lowered away, keyword spellings, operator
  positions, statements without a tree or refused by the wrappers, or the
  acceptance question itself.

  **What the evidence favours.** Route 2 as the oracle for kinds that have a
  tree, plus a small explicit lowering function on our side. It uses the same
  compiled object as production, with no `SQLITE_DEBUG` and no second copy. It
  keeps more of the input than route 1, and its gaps show up as errors.
  - It has to live in the C preamble of `sqlite.go`. cgo drops bit fields and
  turns unions into byte arrays, so Go can't read these structs directly.
  - A build-tagged file can switch it on through package-wide `#cgo CFLAGS`,
  which I tested in a two-file module. Production would then carry none of it.
  - Route 1 could cover what route 2 refuses, as a debug shell run as a separate
  process. That is the same mechanism the tests already use to build the shell.
  - Route 4 only suits a parser that follows `parse.y` rule by rule.
  - The note leaves the decision to Markus.

  **Stability.** Renamed or removed fields break at compile time. For example,
  `SrcItem` lost `pSelect` and `zDatabase` between 3.45.0 and 3.50.0, and
  `TriggerStep` swapped `zTarget` for `pSrc` before 3.53.0. Other changes are
  silent: a union read under the wrong flag (my first walker misread a virtual
  table's union this way), fields the walker doesn't print, and lowering changes
  such as the 3.46.0 `VALUES` change.

  **Where your leads were wrong or imprecise:**
  - `sqlite3ParserTrace` depends on `NDEBUG` rather than `SQLITE_DEBUG`
  directly, but in effect it is debug-only. Its derivation is of Lemon's
  optimized grammar, not of `parse.y` as written.
  - `PARSE_MODE_DECLARE_VTAB` accepts only `CREATE TABLE`.
  - Parameters are refused in views by a `nVar` check and in triggers by the
  fixer.
  - Rename mode does not just keep trees: it changes two of them (the AND-false
  fold and the form of `VALUES`).
  - `create table` trees are partly resolved even in rename mode.
  - Trigger bodies can't hold `WITH`, `DEFAULT VALUES` or `RETURNING` at all.
  - The authorizer cannot reach the `Select`.
  - One wrong lead was my own, not yours: I first thought rename mode skips
  dequoting of column names, and a test showed it does not.

  **Programs**, in
  `/private/tmp/claude-502/-Users-maragubot-Developer-s4ql--claude-worktrees-obj
  ect-store/f1343515-adca-4ca0-9eb0-f507768f8fc7/scratchpad/work/` (not
  committed):
  - `walker/walker.c`, its generated `tknames.inc`, and the inputs `demo.txt`,

  - `dbgshell/` with the debug and non-debug shells and the `t1.sql`–`t5.sql`
