⏺ Agent "Research reading SQLite's parse tree" finished · 26m 9s

  Ran 1 shell command (ctrl+o to expand)

⏺ Yes, the tree can be read out of SQLite's memory, and no patch is needed, so
  the Nix question goes away. The note is on main as
  docs/research/sqlite-parse-tree.md. Nothing is running now, and usage is at
  8%.

  The agent got three routes working on 3.54.1. The one it favours, and I agree,
  is the second:

  - A walker of our own, in the production build. SQLite has a private
    parse-only mode that ALTER TABLE ... RENAME uses. Called from our binding,
    it parses a statement and keeps the trees instead of generating code. A
    select is wrapped as a view, and an insert, update, or delete as a trigger
    body. A small C function then walks the structs and prints them.
  - What it covers: select, insert, update, delete, and create table, index,
    view, and trigger. It keeps quote flags and source positions of names.
  - What has no tree at all: drop, alter, transactions, pragma, and the rest.
    SQLite generates code for those straight from the tokens.
  - What the wrapping refuses, with an error and never silently: parameters, and
    in trigger bodies WITH, RETURNING, and DEFAULT VALUES.

  The other two routes are SQLite's own tree printer and the Lemon parser's
  trace. Both need a debug build, which is a second, different copy of SQLite,
  and the printer loses quote style and positions.

  Two limits to know:

  - It is SQLite's internal form, not a syntax tree. The note lists about 30
    rewrites the grammar has already made, such as LIMIT a, b stored as LIMIT b 
    OFFSET a. So the check is: our tree, lowered by the same rules, equals
    SQLite's. It catches wrong grouping, a clause on the wrong part of a
    compound, and dropped clauses that don't change results.
  - The structs are private. A renamed field breaks at compile time when we bump
    SQLite, but some changes are silent, such as a union read under the wrong
    flag. The agent's own first walker made that mistake.

  This is the first test approach to decide. It adds test-only C to the binding,
  which a build tag can keep out of production builds, and a lowering function
  on our side that mirrors SQLite's rewrites.

  Do you want this tree oracle as one of the parser's tests?

✻ Cooked for 1h 14m 14s · done Monday 11:19 AM

※ recap: We're choosing test approaches for s4ql's own SQL parser before building it; all research is merged. Next: you decide whether to adopt the tree oracle that reads SQLite's parse trees from memory.

❯ Hmm. What do you think, is it worth it? Compared to the other options we      
  have? I think it would be nice if we had an internal tokenizer and parser     
  (into a real AST?) that we have total confidence in, with whatever testing    
  approaches we need.                                                           

⏺ No, I don't think it is worth it now. I'd keep it in reserve and reach total
  confidence with checks that use SQLite only through its public behaviour.

  And yes to a real syntax tree: one that keeps everything as written, with raw
  text, quote style, positions, and keyword spellings. That is itself a reason
  the memory oracle fits badly, because SQLite has no such tree. We would be
  comparing against its internal form through a function of ours that mirrors
  about 30 of its rewrites, and that function is a second place for bugs.

  What can go wrong in a parser, and what catches each:

  - It accepts or rejects wrongly, or gives the wrong message.
    sqlite3_prepare_v2 is an exact oracle for this, over SQLite's own test
    statements, one-token mutations, and fuzzing. The meyer research ran this at
    526,495 statements.
  - It drops something. A property with no oracle catches it: every token of the
    statement must be owned by some node of the tree. This matters most for the
    reject pass, which only has to see that an unlisted construct is there.
  - It groups wrongly. We print our tree back with every operation in
    parentheses and every clause explicit, and SQLite must treat the reprint as
    it treats the original: the same values for expressions, the same bytecode
    from EXPLAIN for statements. Both were run in the research. The first found
    meyer's NOT fault, and the second found rqlite/sql's swapped LIMIT.
  - It is wrong in meaning for something we support. The engine runs the
    statement, and rows and errors are compared with SQLite's.

  What the memory oracle would add is a narrow case: a parser bug and a printer
  bug that cancel each other, such as limit and offset swapped in both. For a
  supported feature the engine comparison catches that. For an unsupported one
  it does no harm, because the statement is rejected either way.

  Its costs are real: test-only C over private structs that can misread silently
  (the agent's own first walker did), the lowering function to keep in step
  with every SQLite upgrade, and gaps for parameters, WITH, and RETURNING.

  One caveat on my side: that the reprint check catches every dropped or
  misplaced clause is my reasoning from two runs, not a measurement. If it
  leaves a gap we can name, the memory oracle is the tool to close it, and the
  research note keeps it ready.

  Shall I record it as "not now, in reserve" and go on to the next approach?

❯ yes                                                                           

⏺ Recorded as "not now, in reserve"; I'll write it into the todo list with the
  other decisions when we are through.

  Second approach: accept or reject, with message and offset

  For every statement in a corpus, our parser and sqlite3_prepare_v2 must agree
  on three things:

  - Whether it parses. A syntax error in SQLite must be one in ours, and the
    reverse.
  - The message, exactly. For example near "X": syntax error, unrecognized 
    token: "X", or incomplete input.
  - The byte offset of the error. This needs sqlite3_error_offset exposed in the
    binding, which is a small addition.

  The corpus is statements extracted from SQLite's own TCL tests (47,418 in the
  rqlite/sql run), its documentation, and its public fuzz corpora. All three are
  public domain. No expected results are stored: the pinned SQLite gives the
  answer when the test runs, so an upgrade of SQLite re-checks everything by
  itself.

  Two known limits, both from the research:

  - Errors raised in the middle of parsing. table t1 already exists comes from a
    grammar action and can hide a syntax error further on. The test runs
    against a schema where names resolve, and skips the statements SQLite
    stopped early (358 of 68,032 in one run, 7,475 of 535,220 in the other).
  - Two faults at one token. SQLite's last message wins there. The meyer agent's
    harness tripped on this once.

  This is the base that everything else stands on, and I see no real alternative
  to it. Do you agree to it, including the exact message and the offset?

✻ Churned for 11s · done Monday 11:39 AM

❯ Yes, sounds good. And feel free to add our own corpus with our own            
  property-based testing, fuzz testing etc.                                     

⏺ Agreed, and noted: our own corpus on top, with property-based tests, fuzzing
  against SQLite, and one-token mutations of valid statements. I'll treat the
  choice of generators as a detail.

  Third approach: nothing is dropped from the tree

  A property that needs no oracle: every token of a statement is owned by
  exactly one node of the tree, and the nodes' spans, put back together in
  order, give the token stream again. Whitespace and comments are the only
  tokens allowed outside a node.

  What it gives us:

  - No construct can be invisible. If the parser read a clause but built nothing
    for it, a token is left without an owner and the test fails. This is what
    the reject pass depends on: it can only refuse what it can see.
  - Names and literals stay as written. A node holds a span of the source, not a
    cleaned-up copy, so quote style, case, and spelling cannot be lost. The
    research showed why this matters: SQLite's table "t1" already exists prints
    the name with its quotes.
  - It runs on every input the parser accepts, including fuzzed ones, because it
    needs nothing from SQLite. The tokens themselves are already checked
    against SQLite.

  What it does not show is that the tokens are owned by the right node. A clause
  attached to the wrong select of a compound still passes. That is the job of
  the next approach.

  It also puts one rule on the design of the tree: every node kind must record
  its span and every keyword it consumed. I think that is a good rule in any
  case.

  Do you agree to this property, and to that rule for the tree?

✻ Cogitated for 10s · done Monday 11:41 AM

❯ I don't understand it all, but yes, I'll trust you on that one                

⏺ Recorded. In one sentence: the test takes the tree apart again and checks that
  every word of the original statement comes back, so the parser cannot have
  silently skipped anything.

  Fourth approach: the tree has the right shape

  This one checks that the parser understood the statement the way SQLite does,
  not only that it kept all the words.

  How it works: we write the statement out again from our tree, with brackets
  around every operation so that nothing is left to precedence rules. For a + b 
  * c our tree gives (a + (b * c)). Then we ask SQLite about both texts:

  - For expressions: SQLite computes both with the same values, and the results
    must be equal. If our parser had grouped it as ((a + b) * c), the results
    differ.
  - For whole statements: SQLite compiles both, and its EXPLAIN output, the
    program it would run, must be the same. A clause that we attached in the
    wrong place or left out changes that program.

  Both were tried in the research, on the other parsers. The first found meyer's
  wrong reading of NOT x NOT NULL in 276,022 random expressions. The second
  found rqlite/sql swapping limit and offset, in 18,286 statements.

  Its limits:

  - It needs a printer, code that writes a tree out as SQL. A bug in the printer
    that exactly undoes a bug in the parser would hide both. For anything the
    engine supports, the comparison of real query results catches that later.
  - It covers what SQLite can compile. Statements with no program to compare,
    and the 67 differences in the rqlite/sql run that came from quoting SQLite
    stores as written, need rules of their own.

  The printer is also useful beyond tests, for error messages and debugging.

  Do you agree to this check?

⏺ That was the last test approach I had. The decisions are recorded in
  docs/todo.md and pushed, and I've started the first builder.

  What is agreed for the parser:

  1. Own parser for all of SQLite's grammar, into a syntax tree that keeps
     everything as written, with no dependency on meyer or rqlite/sql.
  2. Accept or reject, message, and offset against sqlite3_prepare_v2, over
     SQLite's own tests, docs, and fuzz corpora, plus our own corpus with
     property-based tests, fuzzing, and mutations.
  3. Nothing dropped: every token is owned by a node of the tree.
  4. Right shape: the tree written out with full brackets must mean the same to
     SQLite as the original.
  5. In reserve: reading SQLite's tree from its memory.

  I'm building it in two steps:

  - Now running: the corpus and the harness. A builder extracts the corpus from
    the pinned SQLite source, adds the error offset to the binding, and builds
    the test harness that compares any parser with SQLite. It proves the harness
    with stand-in parsers, since ours does not exist yet.
  - After that: the parser itself, with a fresh builder that drives it to zero
    disagreements in that harness.

  Two things I decided as details: once the parser exists, I'll have its code
  mutated to see that the tests notice, as we did for the storage core. And the
  reject pass is a separate piece after the parser, with its own tests.

  The other open decisions (what an error promises, how "not supported" is
  marked, our own API) wait until the parser is built, as you said.

✻ Crunched for 3s · done Monday 12:31 PM

› Message from fabrik:builder
  ## Report: Parser corpus and oracle harness

  **Branch:** `parser-corpus-and-oracle`. It has 9 commits on `c7097dd`,
  including one merge of the writer's README branch
  (`worktree-agent-aa98da491a386da29`). The worktree is clean. The diary is
  `docs/diary/2026-11-16-parser-corpus-and-oracle.md`, with 6 steps.

  `make lint`, `make test`, `make test-asan FUZZTIME=30s` (run in Docker),
  `-race`, and `-count=20` on the new tests all pass. No test depends on timing.
  I did not touch `README.md`, `docs/spec.md` or `docs/roadmap.md`.

  ### Packages
  - **`internal/sqlite`** (the binding)
    - `Error.Offset` comes from `sqlite3_error_offset`, and is -1 when SQLite
  has none.
    - `Conn.PrepareFirst(sql) (*Stmt, tail int, err)` takes any bytes. It
  returns the tail even when it fails, and -1 when SQLite never parsed the text.
  `Prepare` now calls it, so there is one C wrapper.
    - `Conn.DenyProcessPragmas()` is a static C authorizer. It makes
  `soft_heap_limit`, `hard_heap_limit`, `temp_store_directory` and
  `data_store_directory` fail with `not authorized`. SQLite checks this after
  the parse, and these PRAGMAs would otherwise change the whole process when
  prepared.
    - `sqlite.Complete` wraps `sqlite3_complete`.
  - **`internal/sql/oracle`**: `Judge(src) Verdict{Kind, Message, Offset, Code,
  Tail, Reason, Setup}`. `Kind` is `Parses`, `SyntaxError` or `NotSaid`.
  `IsSyntaxMessage(msg)` recognises the three syntax messages.
  - **`internal/sql/corpus`**: the embedded `tcl.txt`, `doc.txt`, `fuzz.txt` and
  `own.txt`, read with `All()`, `Read`, `Encode` and `Decode`. The regeneration
  tool is `corpus/update`, pinned by `source.json`, and `make corpus` runs it.
  - **`internal/sql/parsetest`**:
    - `Parser` / `ParserFunc` / `Result{OK, Message, Offset, Tail}` is the
  interface a parser implements.
    - `Run`, `Test`, `Check` and `Compare` do the comparison. `Report` groups
  disagreements by `Mismatch` (AcceptsInvalid, RejectsValid, Message, Offset,
  Tail) and a key from `Pattern(msg)`, for example `near ID: syntax error` or
  `offset +1`. Each group has a count and its five shortest examples. "Does not
  say" entries are counted apart, by reason and message.
    - `Oracle()` is SQLite itself as a parser. `Mutants(src, seed, n)` makes
  one-token mutations that are reproducible from the seed.
  - `FuzzJudge` and `FuzzMutants` are added to `make fuzz`.
  - `simlint` now exempts `parsetest`, which uses `math/rand/v2` and never runs
  under the simulation.
  - `.gitattributes` keeps LF line endings in the corpus and marks the generated
  files as generated.

  ### How each verdict is decided
  - **Parses**: the statement prepared. Or it failed with a message outside the
  three syntax messages, after the parser had already been handed the
  statement's end. The end is its `;`, or the empty semicolon SQLite feeds at
  the end of the text.
    - It can be told apart because the parse loop stops after the token on which
  an error is raised, and puts the tail after that token. So the token ending
  at the tail is the one, re-tokenized with `internal/sql/token`.
    - If the tail and the last token both end at the end of the text, a second
  prepare with a space appended decides which it was.
    - Only SQLITE_ERROR and SQLITE_AUTH count.
  - **SyntaxError**: the message is `near "X": syntax error`, `incomplete
  input`, or `unrecognized token: "X"`, with SQLITE_ERROR. These come from the
  syntax-error routine, from the parse loop on an unknown token, or from the two
  actions that check tokens (`#N`, and digit separators).
  - **NotSaid**, with a reason:
    - StoppedEarly: an action error or a limit, before the end.
    - InsideTrigger: a `;` in a trigger body. The statement ends only at a `;`
  after `; END`.
    - InsideArguments: a `;` while a parenthesis is open in CREATE VIRTUAL TABLE
  arguments. This is reachable via `too many columns`, and a test covers it.
    - Overwritten: the statement has a `#N` or a misplaced digit separator,
  whose error a later message can replace.
    - Unsettled: the space probe gave no answer.
    - NotParsed: any other result code.
  - **Schema fixes**: CREATE TRIGGER always stops at BEGIN in an empty database,
  and an unknown schema stops at the next token. On those messages, Judge
  creates the missing table, a view for INSTEAD OF, or attaches the schema, then
  judges again. It does this at most 3 times and records the statements in
  `Setup`. The grammar does not depend on the schema.
  - **Connection**: a fresh `:memory:` database for every prepare, with the
  binding's options and default limits, comments on, and process PRAGMAs denied.
  The limits that show up in verdicts are documented in the oracle README:
  PARSER_DEPTH 2500 (2,494 nested parentheses give `Recursion limit`),
  EXPR_DEPTH 1000, VARIABLE_NUMBER 32766, and others.
  - **Checking the rules**: on every corpus entry, and in the fuzz test:
    - every Parses statement with `)` put in place of its end gives `near ")"`
  exactly at that `)`;
    - the text before a syntax error's token has no earlier syntax error;
    - the offset points at the token the message names;
    - bytes after a NUL change nothing.

    All of these hold on 139,234 entries and on 17M fuzz executions.

  ### "Does not say" counts
  On the corpus, SQLite does not say for 308 entries: 295 stopped early, 11
  inside a trigger, 2 overwritten, none unsettled or inside arguments.
  - The largest groups are duplicate column name (78), no such collation
  sequence (49), object name reserved (24), no such column (18) and unknown join
  type (14).
  - With the empty schema alone, 1,687 entries would not be said. The fixes
  settled 1,379, mostly CREATE TRIGGER (about 1,010), CREATE TABLE in another
  schema (189) and ALTER TABLE (167).
  - The other verdicts: 84,215 parse (25,608 prepared, 58,607 failed after the
  parse) and 54,711 are syntax errors.

  ### Corpus
  | Source | Statements | Bytes |
  |---|---|---|
  | tcl (1,541 `.test` files) | 54,599 | 5,232,470 |
  | doc (`<pre>` blocks) | 1,176 | 127,752 |
  | fuzz (`xsql` of `fuzzdata1-8.db`) | 83,247 | 6,048,569 |
  | own (hand-written) | 219 | 6,100 |

  - In total that is 139,234 distinct statements and 11.4 MB, about 1.4 MB with
  gzip.
  - Statements are split with `sqlite3_complete`, trimmed, deduplicated and
  sorted. Statements over 1,000 bytes (tcl, doc) or 128 bytes (fuzz) are left
  out; without the 128-byte cap, fuzz alone was 23.8 MB.
  - To regenerate, run `make corpus`. `-archives DIR` uses local copies,
  `-check` only compares, `-version` re-pins. Two runs gave byte-identical
  output.
  - The archives are checked by size, SHA3-256 and SHA-256. They are public
  domain (sqlite.org/copyright.html).
  - The check against the S3 credentials in `.env` found nothing; only counts
  were printed.

  ### Run times
  Measured on an Apple M4; the asan figures are from Docker Linux arm64.
  - **Plain**: the whole corpus through the oracle takes about 3 s. The oracle
  package takes 4.9–7 s, parsetest 4.7–6.5 s.
  - **`-race`**: oracle about 10 s, parsetest about 10 s.
  - **`-asan`**: on the whole corpus it was 35 s (oracle) and 40 s (parsetest).
  I split it: under asan and with `-short`, the corpus tests use every tenth
  entry, giving 3–9 s and 10–17 s inside `make test-asan`. The plain and race
  runs still use the whole corpus.
  - `Run` is sequential. SQLite's global locks made 10 goroutines take 9.5 s
  against 2.9 s for one.

  ### What the parser builder must know
  Full list in `internal/sql/oracle/README.md`; the main points:
  - **Offset -1**:
    - always for `incomplete input` and for misplaced digit separators. Their
  message shows the number partly rewritten: `1__2__3` gives `"12_2__3"`;
    - for `#N` only when it is raised at the end of the text: `select #1` gives
  -1, `select #1 from t` gives 7.
  - **When those two checks run**: when the next token arrives, and a syntax
  error on that token wins: `select 1_ 2` gives `near "2"`.
  - **The last message on a token wins**: `create table t (x) foo bar` gives
  `near "bar"`.
  - **Tails**:
    - `unrecognized token` has its tail at the token's start, not after it;
    - a statement that parses has its tail after its `;` or at the end of the
  text, and leading empty statements are skipped.
  - **NUL**: a NUL ends the text and ends a string inside it: `'a\0b'` gives
  `unrecognized token: "'a"`. `/*` as the last two bytes, or just before a NUL,
  is two tokens.
  - **Errors for the pass after the parser**: some errors come from actions and
  are not the parser's job, such as `syntax error after column name "a"`,
  `unknown table option`, and `ORDER BY ... before UNION`.

  `docs/todo.md` is updated with this briefly.

  ### Self-review
  The code-review skill (high) gave ten findings. I fixed seven:
  - a quoted or bracketed attached schema was never matched again;
  - `Encode` depended on `unicode.IsPrint`, so the files could change with the
  Go version; it now uses a fixed list;
  - the canonical-form test was a no-op; the new one caught that `own.txt`
  escaped `日`;
  - `-check -version` rewrote `source.json`;
  - CRLF checkouts would break every corpus line;
  - `Pattern` took unused arguments;
  - the prepare wrapper was duplicated.

  I declined three:
  - attaching the schema for "trigger cannot reference objects in database":
  SQLite rejects that whatever the schema holds; a test now pins it;
  - using `sqlite3_complete` for triggers: it wrongly treats `select 1 as end;`
  inside a body as the end;
  - sharing connections: each verdict is about 10 µs, and a fresh connection
  keeps verdicts independent.

  ### Unsure or not done
  - No property-based library was added. The repo has none; mutations and
  fuzzing cover generation for now.
  - The `Unsettled` reason has no test, because no input I found reaches it.
  - `corpus/update -check` is not in CI, since it downloads 27 MB.
  - `internal/core` builds two injected `sqlite.Error` values without `Offset`,
  so they get 0. Nothing reads it.
  - The oracle depends on `internal/sql/token` for re-tokenizing; that package
  is fuzz-verified against `sqlite3GetToken`.
  - The branch has one merge commit, if you prefer linear history.

  **Key files**: `/Users/maragubot/Developer/s4ql/.claude/worktrees/agent-a51d10
  4c0082de93e/internal/sql/oracle/oracle.go`,
  `.../internal/sql/parsetest/parsetest.go`,
  `.../internal/sql/parsetest/mutate.go`, `.../internal/sql/corpus/corpus.go`,
  `.../internal/sql/corpus/update/main.go`, `.../internal/sqlite/sqlite.go`, and
  the three READMEs in `.../internal/sql/{oracle,corpus,parsetest}/README.md`.

⏺ Agent "SQL parser" is waiting on its own background work · 1h 36m 35s

⏺ Agent "SQL parser" is waiting on its own background work · 2h 15m 36s

※ recap: We are building s4ql's own SQL parser, checked against SQLite; the test harness is merged, and the parser builder is still running, waiting on its own background job. Next I review its report and merge when the checks pass. (disable recaps in /config)

› Message from fabrik:builder
  ## Report: SQL parser (branch `sql-parser`, 28 commits on `origin/main`
  b9c8f9f, worktree
  `/Users/maragubot/Developer/s4ql/.claude/worktrees/agent-a98257c88bff85800`)

  The parser and its tree are built. The corpus, the mutants, the generator, and
  the fuzzer all end at zero disagreements with SQLite. `make lint`, `make
  test`, and `make test-asan` pass. A writer sub-agent's branch
  `worktree-agent-a6698d0d5d5e6f582` (the README) is fast-forward merged into
  `sql-parser`. Its worktree at `.claude/worktrees/agent-a6698d0d5d5e6f582` can
  be removed.

  ### Packages and API
  - **`internal/sql/ast`**: the tree, 113 node types, each doc-commented with
  its `parse.y` rules.
    - Groups: `Expr`, `Cmd`, `SelectCore`, `FromSource`, `JoinConstraint`,
  `ColumnConstraint`, `TableConstraint`, `AlterAction`.
    - Root: `Statement{Explain, Query, Plan, Cmd, Semi}`.
    - `Token{Kind, Offset, Text}`: the zero value is absent, and `Kind` is
  always the tokenizer's kind, also for keywords read as names.
    - Functions: `Span`, `Children`, `Tokens`, `Walk`, `AllTokens`, `SetSpan`,
  `Dump` (a one-line text form for tests), `CheckOwnership`.
  - **`internal/sql/parser`**:
    - `Parse(src) (*ast.Statement, tail int, error)` reads the first statement,
  as `sqlite3_prepare_v2` does.
    - `ParseAll(src)` reads every statement in turn.
    - `Tokens(src, stmt)` gives the tokenizer's tokens for a statement, for the
  ownership check.
    - `Error{Message, Offset, Limit}`, `MaxDepth = 2500`, `MaxLength = 1e9`.
    - Hand-written recursive descent with precedence climbing; every non-trivial
  function names its rules.
  - The README is `internal/sql/parser/README.md`.

  ### How tokens and ownership work
  - Every token a node consumes is a `Token` or `[]Token` field, and every node
  has a `Span` that the parser sets.
  - `Children` and `Tokens` read any node's fields through reflection, with a
  plan cached per type. Any field that is not a token, a node, or a struct of
  those panics, so nothing can hide from the walk.
  - An internal test parses the `ast` sources with `go/parser`, so the list of
  node types cannot go stale.
  - `CheckOwnership` checks four things:
    - every token read is owned exactly once;
    - the tree's tokens in order are the statement's tokens;
    - spans cover exactly a node's own tokens and its children;
    - siblings do not overlap.
  - It runs on every statement that parses in all the test layers and both fuzz
  tests. `TestCheckOwnership` proves it on 8 kinds of deliberately broken tree.

  ### Final counts
  - **Corpus:** 139,234 statements; 138,926 agree, SQLite does not say on 308, 0
  disagree.
  - **Mutants:** 416,271 (3 per entry) in the normal run, and one long run of
  5,550,280 (40 per entry); 0 disagree.
  - **Generator:** reads `testdata/parse.y` (all 412 rules, checked against the
  amalgamation's rule list). The default is 60,000 texts; a long run compared
  1,200,000. 0 disagree.
  - **Fuzzing:** `FuzzParse` made 767,903,697 executions over three runs (352M,
  then 135M, then 280M after the fix). `FuzzGenerate` made 16,059,438. Both are
  in `make fuzz`.
  - **Rule coverage:** I run SQLite's own LALR automaton, read from the
  amalgamation's tables, beside the parser.
    - It reduces 395 of 412 rules for the corpus, and 398 with the generator.
    - The other 14 are code-less unit rules that Lemon folds into gotos, so no
  automaton ever reduces them. Each is used in the derivation of a generated
  sentence that parses.
    - The automaton agrees with the parser on acceptance and on the error token
  for every entry.
  - **Statement coverage:** `parser` is at 99.5%. The 5 uncovered spots are:
    - the default case of `tableConstraint`, whose caller checks the token
  first;
    - two guards that only a bug can reach;
    - the empty-token guard in `Tokens`;
    - a lookahead past EOF that no rule needs.
  - **Hand-written tests:** shape tests for 88 expression forms and 66
  statements, 34 pinned edge cases, and about 200 corner texts checked against
  SQLite.

  ### Limits
  - **Depth (`MaxDepth` 2500).** A level is counted per expression operand, and
  per parenthesised SELECT or join outside expressions.
    - Each level holds at least one entry of SQLite's parser stack. The tests
  check the depth never exceeds that stack, against the automaton on the corpus,
  the generator, and 14 kinds of deep nesting.
    - SQLite allows 2,499 stack entries (2,493 nested parentheses parse). We
  parse up to 2,499 parentheses and reject 2,500. The limit never rejects what
  SQLite parses, and affects no corpus entry.
    - A million nested parentheses fail at once.
  - **Length:** 1e9 bytes of tokens, counted per parse, as SQLite counts per
  prepare.

  ### Run times and benchmark (Apple M4; asan in Docker)
  - The package takes about 10 s plain, 10 s with `-race`, and about 14–20 s
  under asan. `-race` and asan use every tenth corpus entry, as the harness
  does.
  - `-count=20`: parser 385 s, ast 0.1 s, oracle 208 s.
  - `BenchmarkParse`: about 1.4 µs and 22 allocations per corpus statement, 50
  MB/s.
  - `BenchmarkParse_long` keeps a constant MB/s from 1,000 to 100,000 items for
  lists, chains, VALUES, many statements, and nesting. Nothing is quadratic.

  ### Where SQLite surprised me
  - **GENERATED is a type word.** Lemon tries the fallback to ID only for a
  token without its own action, and default reductions are not listed as
  actions. So `a int generated always as (1)` has the type `int generated
  always`. I found these 4 states with a patched Lemon report; GENERATED is the
  only such keyword.
  - **The first table option may be empty:** `create table t(a), strict` parses.
  - **BETWEEN:** the AND is shifted into a state merged with `expr AND expr`.
  The low operand runs over every operator but a top-level AND, so `x BETWEEN a
  OR b AND c` fails.
  - **NOT-led operators** have NOT's precedence 3, because a rule takes the
  precedence of its leftmost terminal that has one.
  - **Fallback keywords that stay keywords:**
    - CAST, RAISE, and CTIME_KW at an expression start;
    - WITH after `(` that can start a SELECT, but not in function arguments;
    - CURRENT and UNBOUNDED at a frame bound;
    - PARTITION, RANGE, ROWS, and GROUPS at a window's start;
    - RECURSIVE after WITH;
    - WITHOUT among table options.
  - **The `#N` offset** is -1 when the error comes with the final semicolon of a
  SELECT, CREATE VIEW, ATTACH, or DETACH. Their actions read the schema even
  after an error, which runs SQL of SQLite's own and resets the offset.
    - The offset stays if SQLite already read the schema while parsing. A
  multi-row VALUES with constant rows and no CTE before it does that: it builds
  a co-routine.
    - Getting this exact needed SQLite's `exprIsConst` (`constant.go`) and a
  table of the binding's 159 built-in function overloads (`functions.go`). The
  table is checked against `pragma_function_list` and against SQLite's own
  decision for every function. The fuzzer found this case.
  - **Others:** `select 1 end` parses (END is an alias). An ON after a FROM item
  is always the join's, by `%nonassoc ON` against `[OR]`. Lemon renumbers
  rules, putting those with code first.

  ### Fault found and fixed in the oracle
  - `create view aux.v as select #1` gave a SyntaxError at offset 28, but only
  after the oracle attached `aux`. In the empty database, `unknown database aux`
  overwrites the error, and the offset depends on whether the schema was read.
  - `oracle.Judge` now gives NotSaid/Overwritten for a `#N` SyntaxError found
  with setup, when the parse reached the statement's end. A `#N` inside a
  trigger step still counts.
  - Two tests, and the oracle README updated. No corpus count changed: NotSaid
  is still 308.
  - I found no fault in the corpus, the harness, or the tokenizer.

  ### Action errors left to the pass after the parser
  The full list, from a sourced research sub-agent and partly verified against
  SQLite, is in the README under "Errors left to the pass after the parser":
  - about 90 messages raised while the statement is read, each with its SQLite
  file:line and schema/limit marks;
  - the timing rules;
  - the command-level functions that run on the final semicolon.

  Examples: `unknown table option`, `syntax error after column name`, `%s clause
  should come after %s not before`, `duplicate column name`, `unknown join
  type`, `a JOIN clause is required before %s`, and the trigger, window, and
  VALUES errors.

  ### Other changes
  - `internal/sql/corpus/update` now copies `src/parse.y` from the verified
  archive to `internal/sql/parser/testdata/parse.y`, and `-check` checks it.
  - `docs/todo.md`: built items removed; the pass after the parser added, with
  notes for its builder.
  - `Makefile`: `internal/sql/parser` added to `make fuzz`.
  - The oracle and parsetest READMEs now point at the parser.
  - The diary is `docs/diary/2026-11-16-sql-parser.md`, steps 1–5.

  ### Self-review
  The code-review skill (high) found 8 issues; all are fixed:
  - the README was missing (the writer added it);
  - rule coverage now requires a folded rule to be a code-less unit rule;
  - `parser.Tokens` is now linear;
  - `docs/todo.md` no longer lists built work;
  - the length-limit message now formats its limit;
  - three code cleanups.

  ### Unsure, or not done
  - **Schema-read offset table:** the `#N` decision after a multi-row VALUES
  depends on which functions are registered. The table matches the binding as it
  is; an application-defined function would change that one offset.
  - **Unmatched SQLite rejection:** texts nested 2,494–2,499 deep parse in our
  parser, where SQLite says `Recursion limit` (NotSaid). The pass after the
  parser could reproduce that.
  - **Error list:** it is researched from source and only partly verified by
  experiment.
  - **Unexplained asan leak:** one `make test-asan` run reported a LeakSanitizer
  leak in the parser test binary after PASS. It came while the review agent had
  a scratch test in the package and while I was editing. I could not reproduce
  it with any test alone, with the failing shuffle seed, or in two later full
  runs, which passed.
  - **Scratchpad dependency:** the patched-Lemon analysis that found the
  GENERATED states lives only in the scratch directory. In the repository,
  `TestRuleCoverage` checks the parser against the real tables instead.

› Message from fabrik:builder
  ## Summary

  **Branch:** `parser-no-codegen-emulation`, in
  /Users/maragubot/Developer/s4ql/.claude/worktrees/agent-aec01097a7fda10e0. It
  has 6 commits on top of `20acdfa` (the tip of `sql-parser`). While I worked,
  `origin/sql-parser` disappeared: PR #23 was merged into `origin/main` as
  `5238b88`, and `20acdfa` is in `origin/main`. `git merge-tree --write-tree
  origin/main HEAD` shows a clean merge. The lead can merge the branch into
  `main` as it is, or rebase it onto `main`. Not pushed.

  The parser no longer imitates SQLite's code generator. The oracle now marks
  the `#N` offsets that depend on whether SQLite read the schema, and the
  harness skips the offset for those texts. Every check you asked for passes
  with zero disagreements, and there was no LeakSanitizer report.

  ## What was removed

  - Deleted 574 lines in 4 files: `internal/sql/parser/constant.go` (270),
  `functions.go` (177), `constant_test.go` (63) and `functions_test.go` (64).
  - Removed the fields `withAt` and `schemaReadAt` from `parser.go`, and the
  `valuesRow` call and the `withAt` bookkeeping from `select.go`.
  - `export_test.go` is back as it was before `117c471` (`BuiltinFunctions` is
  gone).
  - `git diff 117c471^ -- internal/sql/parser/*.go` is empty, so the parser's Go
  code is exactly what it was before the emulation. The simple `#N` rule
  (`resetsOffset`, `endsAt`) and the timing of the pending error are kept.
  - In total the branch changes 17 files: 481 lines added and 635 deleted.

  ## The oracle's new condition

  `oracle.Verdict` has a new field, `OffsetNotSaid bool`. The verdict stays a
  `SyntaxError`, and `Offset` still holds what SQLite gave. `Judge` sets the
  field through `schema.offsetNotSaid` when the error is a `#N` error and all
  three of these hold:

  1. **One of the four commands, not led by WITH.** After EXPLAIN or EXPLAIN
  QUERY PLAN, the first token is SELECT, VALUES, ATTACH or DETACH, or the
  statement starts with CREATE [TEMP] VIEW.
  2. **A multi-row VALUES.** Some VALUES is followed by `(`, and the `)` that
  matches it is followed by `,` and `(`. This is checked on the tokens SQLite
  read up to the tail, with comments and leading empty statements skipped.
  3. **The error came with the statement's end.** I reuse `reachedEnd`: only
  `StoppedEarly`, `InsideTrigger` and `InsideArguments` count as "before the
  end". `Unsettled` and `NotParsed` count as the end, to stay on the wide side.

  **Why it covers every case.** Each condition is necessary for SQLite to keep
  an offset that the end would otherwise lose:
  - The parse loop stops on the token whose action raised the error, so an error
  before the end has the token's offset whatever the schema.
  - I read the callers of `sqlite3ReadSchema` in `internal/sqlite/c/sqlite3.c`.
  Only `sqlite3MultiValues` runs before the end of one of the four commands, and
  it is reached only from the two `mvalues` rules, which need a second row.
  - A statement-initial WITH is always the keyword, and `withnm ::= nm` sets
  `bHasWith` before any VALUES row after it. So `sqlite3MultiValues` never reads
  the schema in a statement that starts with WITH.
  - The removed emulation only changed an answer when `valuesRow` set
  `schemaReadAt`. That needed a second row, `resetsOffset` and `endsAt`, and the
  condition covers each of those.

  **Rule for `#N` with setup:** I kept it as it was (`NotSaid`, `Overwritten`)
  and did not fold it into the new field. In the empty database another message
  replaces that error (`unknown database aux`), so even the message is not
  SQLite's verdict there. Marking it as a `SyntaxError` with the offset unsaid
  would claim more than the oracle knows.

  **`parsetest`:**
  - `Compare` checks the message (and acceptance) and skips the offset when the
  field is set.
  - `Report.OffsetNotSaid` counts the texts that agree on everything else; they
  are not in `Agreed`, and `Disagreements` subtracts them.
  - They are listed in `NotSaidGroups` under `offset not said: <pattern>`, but
  `NotSaid` does not count them. The doc comment says so.
  - The report line now reads "…, SQLite does not say on N, nor the offset on
  M".

✻ Cogitated for 19s · done Monday 5:19 PM

❯ Could you explain the offset issue to me? I don't understand                  

⏺ It is about one number that SQLite reports with a syntax error: the position
  in the text where the error is.

  What the offset is. For select 1 frm t, SQLite says near "t": syntax error and
  also gives the byte position of t, here 13. We agreed that our parser must
  match the message and this position.

  The odd construct. SQLite has a kind of variable written #1, #2, and so on,
  that only its own internals may use. If you write it yourself, you always get
  a syntax error:

  - select #1 from t gives the error with position 7, where #1 starts.
  - select #1 gives the same error with position -1, meaning "no position".

  Why -1. When the error is found at the very end of a select, SQLite still does
  some end-of-statement work, and part of that is loading the database's
  schema. Loading the schema runs a query inside SQLite, and that inner query
  wipes the stored error position. So -1 is not a decision about the SQL; it is
  a side effect.

  The corner the fuzzer found. If SQLite had already loaded the schema earlier
  in the same statement, the wipe does not happen and the real position
  survives. That happens for a values list with several rows, but only if every
  row is "constant" by SQLite's internal rules, for example:

  - values(0),(0) union select #0 gives position 27, not -1.

  What the builder did. To match that, it taught our parser SQLite's internal
  rules for "is this expression constant", which needs the list of all 159
  built-in functions. That is 450 lines copying SQLite's code generator, inside
  a parser, to get one number right for SQL nobody writes.

  What I changed. I had that removed. Our parser now uses the simple rule
  (position -1 at the end of those statements). For the narrow case where
  SQLite's answer depends on the schema loading, the oracle says "SQLite's
  position is not meaningful here", and the test skips the position there while
  still comparing the message. No statement in the corpus is affected.

  The trade is that in that one corner our position can differ from SQLite's (-1
  where SQLite says 27).

✻ Brewed for 1h 7m 45s · done Monday 5:20 PM

❯ Is that a bug in SQLite or a documented quirk?                                
  Searched for 1 pattern (ctrl+o to expand)

⏺ Neither clearly; I would call it an undocumented inconsistency, closer to a
  small bug than to a quirk.

  The documentation of sqlite3_error_offset (in the header we vendor) says: if
  the error references a specific token, it returns the byte offset of that
  token; if it does not, it returns -1. Here the error does reference a token,
  since the message is near "#1": syntax error, yet the offset is -1. So SQLite
  does not do what its documentation says in this case. Nothing in the
  documentation mentions the schema loading or the values exception.

  It is harmless in practice: the message is right, the statement is rejected
  either way, and #1 is not something anyone writes. I have not looked in
  SQLite's bug tracker or forum for it, and following your earlier instruction I
  would not report it without your say-so.

  ## Is it known?

  No report found. I found nothing on the `-1` when the statement ends on `#N`,
  and nothing on a fresh connection versus one whose schema is loaded.
  (verified, searches below)

  What I searched:
  - **SQLite forum, its own search:** `sqlite3_error_offset` (12 hits), `error
  offset` (79 hits), `errByteOffset` (0 hits), `syntax error offset -1`. None
  are about this.
    - The closest is the thread "sqlite3_error_offset(db)", 2023-03-12,
  https://sqlite.org/forum/forumpost/45def8cd17. The user got -1 for "no such
  table". Keith Medcalf wrote: "Not all errors appear to trigger the setting of
  an offset though." No developer answered on why.
  - **SQLite bug forum** (https://sqlite.org/bugs/search?s=error_offset):
  nothing relevant.
  - **Ticket [33aa4c0de8a62e33] and check-in pages on sqlite.org/src:** blocked
  behind a login challenge. I used the GitHub mirror `sqlite/sqlite` for history
  instead.
  - **GitHub issues and PRs mentioning `"sqlite3_error_offset"`** (150 hits;
  read the top 50, plus a search for `error_offset sqlite -1`). Nothing on this.
    - Related but the opposite fault: bun PR oven-sh/bun#45980 (open,
  2026-10-18) reports an old offset that survives into a later error.
  - **Web search** for `-1`, syntax error, first statement, schema: nothing.

  ## The cause is right, with one correction

  All verified, in the vendored `sqlite3.c`:
  - The `expr ::= VARIABLE` action (line 185338) calls `parserSyntaxError`. That
  calls `sqlite3ErrorMsg` (37090), which records the offset.
  - When `#N` is the last token, the parser reduces all the way up to `cmd ::=
  select` (line 184843) in the same call. That action runs `sqlite3ReadSchema`
  unless `DBFLAG_EncodingFixed` is set. `sqlite3Select` would have returned
  early on `nErr`, but it is never reached first.
  - The path that clears the offset is `sqlite3ReadSchema` → `sqlite3Init` →
  `sqlite3InitOne` → `sqlite3_exec` (148824) / `sqlite3InitCallback` → nested
  `sqlite3Prepare` (148577). Its successful end calls `sqlite3ErrorClear`
  (149271), which sets `errByteOffset=-1`. I confirmed this with a backtrace
  from an instrumented copy in the scratch directory.
  - ATTACH and DETACH reach the same reset through `codeAttach` →
  `sqlite3ReadSchema` (126235). CREATE VIEW reaches it through
  `sqlite3StartTable` (128120).
  - `sqlite3MultiValues` (140044) loads the schema earlier in the parse, so the
  later read does nothing. That explains `values(0),(0) union select #0`.

  Correction: the SELECT case is gated on `DBFLAG_EncodingFixed`, not on whether
  the schema is loaded. In practice both are set by the first schema load.

  ## It depends on the connection being fresh (verified by experiment)

  The same statement gives -1 on a new connection, and the real offset after one
  earlier `select 1`:

  | Statement | Fresh | After `select 1` |
  |---|---|---|
  | `select #1` | -1 | 7 |
  | `create view v as select #1` | -1 | 24 |
  | `detach #1` | -1 | 7 |
  | `select 1 where #1` | -1 | 15 |
  | `with c as (select 1) select #2` | -1 | 28 |
  | `explain select #1` | -1 | 15 |

  It is not only `#N`. Any token error raised in a grammar action on the last
  token behaves the same way:
  - `select ?0` and `select ?99999999` ("variable number must be between ?1 and
  ?32766"): -1 fresh, 7 after.
  - `select f(1,1,…)` with 1100 arguments ("too many arguments on function f"):
  -1 fresh, 7 after.

  That is the more visible form for users: errors from `?0` or too many
  arguments lose their offset on the first statement of a connection. (verified)

  Unrelated finding: `select 1_` and `select 1__0` ("unrecognized token") give
  -1 both fresh and after a load, but `select 0x_1` gives 7. (verified)

  ## Related history

  All verified on the GitHub mirror unless marked:
  - **Added:** 3.38.0 (2022-02-22), check-in a98c868a10, 2021-12-25. The release
  note says it "can sometimes help to localize an SQL error".
  - **Offset suppressed for errors in triggers and views:** e1c4743121
  (2022-02-07) and 9566982328 (2024-11-20, which added `test/errofst1.test`).
  - **Out-of-range values on generated columns:** fixed in 3.41.2 (715963f1ba,
  6263cefcf5, 0d53e8ed54, 2023-03-20), ticket 33aa4c0de8.
  - **Where the SELECT form comes from:** `bc60d7bee9` (2024-11-04), the fix for
  forum thread bc75a4d20b756044, added the `sqlite3ReadSchema` call to `cmd ::=
  select`. It was first released in 3.48.0; 3.47.0 to 3.47.2 do not have it.
    - So the SELECT form most likely started in 3.48.0. (inferred, not tested on
  3.47)
    - The ATTACH, DETACH and CREATE VIEW forms are probably older. (inferred)
  - **Trunk after 3.54.1, to 2026-11-16:** no change to `errByteOffset`,
  `sqlite3ErrorClear`, or `cmd ::= select`. I checked commits to `prepare.c`,
  `util.c`, `main.c`, `parse.y`, `insert.c`, `attach.c` and `printf.c`, and
  grepped the trunk files. The only change related to `sqlite3_error_offset` is
  5b6097d87f (2026-06-25), which takes the mutex.
  - `errofst1.test` tests only the view case.

  ## How firm the contract is

  I found no statement by a SQLite developer that it is "best effort". The
  documentation in `c3ref/errcode.html` has no hedging. The only soft wording is
  "can sometimes help" in the 3.38.0 release note. (verified)

  The scratch files are in `/private/tmp/claude-502/-Users-maragubot-Developer-s
  4ql/f1343515-adca-4ca0-9eb0-f507768f8fc7/scratchpad/offset-research/`: `t.c`
  and `v.c` are the experiments, and `s.c` is the instrumented copy.

⏺ Not known, as far as a careful search can tell, and it is broader than I told
  you.

  - No report found. Nothing in SQLite's forum, bug forum, or the trackers of
    projects that use the offset. The closest is a 2023 forum thread where a
    user got -1 for "no such table" and no developer explained why. SQLite's
    ticket pages were behind a login challenge, so the agent read the source
    history on the GitHub mirror instead.
  - It depends on whether the connection is fresh. On a new connection select #1
    gives -1; after any earlier statement such as select 1, the same text gives
    the real position, 7. The agent confirmed this by experiment and traced it
    in the source: the first schema load on a connection clears the stored
    position.
  - It is not only #1. Any error raised on the last token behaves the same on a
    fresh connection, for example select ?0 ("variable number must be between ?1
    and ?32766") and a function call with too many arguments.
  - Age. The select form most likely started in 3.48.0, when a fix added the
    schema load to that grammar rule. That is inferred from the history, not
    tested. Trunk after 3.54.1 has no change to it.
  - Contract. No SQLite developer has called the offset "best effort"; the only
    soft wording is "can sometimes help" in the 3.38.0 release note.

  So it reads as a real, unreported, minor bug: the first statement on a
  connection can lose its error position.

  This changes what I do next. Our oracle opens a fresh connection for every
  verdict, so the -1 we matched is itself the artifact. I plan a small
  follow-up: the oracle warms its connection first so SQLite gives the
  documented position, and our parser then always reports the real position of
  #1. That should remove both the special rule in the parser and the "offset not
  said" marker from #24.

› Message from fabrik:builder
  ## Summary

  The oracle now loads the schema on every connection before it prepares the
  text. After that, every `#N` error in the corpus and its mutants has the
  offset of its token, and the parser's rule and `Verdict.OffsetNotSaid` are
  gone. Over the corpus, the only verdicts that changed are offsets that went
  from -1 to the token's offset. All checks pass, with zero disagreements.

  Branch: **`oracle-warm-connection`**. It is six commits on `4a580fe` and is
  not pushed.

  ## How the connection is warmed, and why that is certain

  In `schema.prepare` in `internal/sql/oracle/oracle.go`, the new `loadSchema`
  prepares and finalizes `select 1`. It runs after the setup statements and
  before the text. `prepare` is the only path, so this covers the first prepare,
  the prepares with setup or attached databases, the probe with a space
  appended, and the prepare per digit separator.

  Why it is certain, from `sqlite3.c`:
  - `cmd ::= select` calls `sqlite3ReadSchema` unless `DBFLAG_EncodingFixed` is
  set.
  - Only reading a row of a schema sets that flag (the call at the start of
  `sqlite3InitOne` is masked back afterwards). In an empty database the SELECT
  therefore always runs `sqlite3Init`, which loads main, temp, and every
  attached database.
  - `sqlite3ReadSchema` does nothing once every database is loaded.
  - `attachFunc` calls `sqlite3Init` itself, and `OP_ParseSchema` for CREATE
  TABLE or VIEW adds to the loaded schema without clearing it. So a setup never
  unloads, and the warm-up after it is either a load or a no-op.

  `TestSchema_prepare` in `oracle_internal_test.go` measures both a fresh
  connection and the oracle's connection:

  | Statement | Fresh connection | Oracle's connection |
  | --- | --- | --- |
  | `select #1` | -1 | 7 |
  | `create view v as select #1` | -1 | 24 |
  | `detach #1` | -1 | 7 |
  | `select 1 where #1` | -1 | 15 |
  | `with c as (select 1) select #2` | -1 | 28 |
  | `explain select #1` | -1 | 15 |
  | `attach 'x' as #1` | -1 | 14 |
  | `values(0),(a) union select #0` | -1 | 27 |
  | `values(0),(0) union select #0` | 27 | 27 |
  | `select ?0` | -1 | 7 |
  | `delete from t where #1` | 20 | 20 |

  It also covers a table, a trigger on a table, a view, a table in temp, and an
  attached schema. With setup, the fresh connection already gives the token's
  offset, because the setup statements load the schema.

  ## What was removed

  - **Parser**: `resetsOffset`, `endsAt`, the `pendingError.variable` field, and
  the `stmt` argument of `parser.result`, in `parser.go` and `expr.go` (35
  lines out, 5 in). The timing of the pending `#N` and digit-separator checks is
  unchanged.
  - **Oracle**: `Verdict.OffsetNotSaid`, `offsetNotSaid`,
  `readsSchemaAfterError`, and `hasMultiRowValues` (`oracle.go`: 108 lines out,
  37 in, including `loadSchema` and the rewritten doc comment of `Judge`).
  `commandStart` and `kindAt` stay, because `insideStatement` uses them.
  - **Harness**: the skip in `Compare`, `Report.OffsetNotSaid`, its "offset not
  said" groups, and its part of the report header (`parsetest.go`: 17 lines out,
  5 in).

  **Kept: the setup rule.** A `#N` error found only with setup, at the
  statement's end, is still `NotSaid`, `Overwritten`. The reason is the message:
  in the empty database, `create view aux.v as select #1` still gives `unknown
  database aux` on the same end token, which replaces the `#N` error.
  `TestSchema_prepare` pins this. The rule is also conservative in one way, as
  before: `create table aux.t as select #1` stops early in the empty database,
  but is `NotSaid` with setup. I left that as it was.

  ## Verdicts that changed

  I dumped every verdict (kind, message, offset, code, tail, reason,
  `OffsetNotSaid`, setup) for the corpus and three mutants of each entry,
  555,505 texts. The baseline was the old code: the same build with the warm-up
  switched off. There were no changes in kind, message, code, tail, reason, or
  setup.

  - **Corpus: 16 changed offsets.** 13 are `#N` syntax errors, for example
  `select #1` from -1 to 7 and `DETACH x IS #1` from -1 to 12. The other 3 are
  `Parses` verdicts with the message `variable number must be between ?1 and
  ?32766` (`select ?0`, `select ?32767`, `select ?99999999999999999999`), each
  from -1 to 7. That message was not in the brief; it is not a syntax message,
  and the harness does not compare offsets of `Parses`.
  - **Mutants: 6 changed offsets**, all `#N` syntax errors.
  - No text had `OffsetNotSaid` set before the change.

  ## Cases still without the token's offset

  None found. On the warm connection, no `#N` error in the corpus or the mutants
  has the offset -1. The only syntax errors with -1 are `incomplete input`
  (2,812 in the corpus) and a misplaced digit separator (11).

  I added a property to the oracle's `check`: a `near "X"` syntax error always
  has an offset. `TestJudge_corpus` and `FuzzJudge` now enforce it, and
  `FuzzJudge` found no exception.

  One property needed relaxing. "A syntax error is the first" had passed only
  because the prefix's `#N` error had -1. In `select #1 2` the error is `near
  "2"` at 10, and the prefix `select #1 ` gives the `#1` error at 7, which is
  SQLite's own behaviour. The property now allows a `#N` that is the last token
  of the prefix (`lastVariable` in `oracle_test.go`).

  ## Counts

  - **Corpus**: 139,234 texts, 138,926 agree, SQLite does not say on 308, 0

  - **Mutants**: 416,271 texts, 415,526 agree, 745 not said, 0 disagreements.
  - **Generator**: 60,000 texts, 54,643 agree, 5,357 not said, 0 disagreements.
  - **`TestParse_offsetAfterValues`**: 1,034 texts. 1,008 give a syntax error,

✻ Brewed for 18s · done Monday 7:25 PM

※ recap: We are building s4ql's own SQL parser, checked against SQLite; the parser is merged and agrees with SQLite everywhere, and a builder is now checking the tree's shape. Next I review and merge that work when it reports.

› Message from fabrik:builder
  ## Summary

  The shape check is built on branch **`tree-shape-check`**, and **it found no
  fault in the parser's tree.** It ran on the whole corpus, every ordered pair
  of operator forms plus 20,000 triples, 66 statements written by hand, and
  about 313 million fuzz executions. I damaged trees on purpose and the check
  catches it, so it does fail on a wrong shape. Every case it misses is one
  SQLite itself treats as the same program; the list is below.

  `make test`, `make lint` and `make test-asan` pass. The new tests also pass
  with `-race -count=20`. Nothing is pushed. The branch starts at `20acdfa`, the
  old tip of `sql-parser`. That commit is now in `origin/main`, since #23 was
  merged during the run, so the branch only needs `main`'s two later README and
  site commits.

  ## Packages and API

  New package `internal/sql/shapetest` (`print.go`, `check.go`, `schema.go`,
  `report.go`, `generate.go`, and its README):
  - `Print(n ast.Node) string` writes a tree from its structure.
  - `Check(src) Result`, and `Checker` for many statements in turn.
    - `Result` holds `Way` (`Bytecode`, `Probes`, `SameError`, `Skipped`),
  `Reason`, one `ExprResult` per expression, and `Fault`.
  - `Run`, `Summarize`, `Test(t, sqls)` and `Report` aggregate results.
  - `Forms` (61 operator forms), `Pairs(seed, n)` and `Triples(seed, n)`
  generate test expressions.

  Changes outside the package:
  - **New binding call:** `sqlite.Conn.SetLengthLimit(n)` wraps
  `sqlite3_limit(SQLITE_LIMIT_LENGTH)`, with a test and a README line. It is the
  one C-side change; please look at it.
  - **simlint:** `shapetest` is exempt in `internal/simlint/simlint.go`, as
  `parsetest` is, because it uses `math/rand/v2`.
  - **Makefile:** `make fuzz` now runs `FuzzCheck`.
  - **Docs:** `docs/todo.md` and one line each in the parser and oracle READMEs.
  - **Diary:** `docs/diary/2026-11-16-tree-shape-check.md`, 5 steps.

  ## Where the printer adds parentheses, and where it cannot

  `parse.y` drops parentheses (`expr ::= LP expr RP {A = X;}`), so wrapping is
  neutral. `select a+b*c from t where a order by 1` and the same with every
  operation in parentheses give identical bytecode, and `ORDER BY (1)` is still
  column 1.

  What it writes:
  - **Wrapped:** every unary, binary, IS, NULL test, LIKE/GLOB/REGEXP/MATCH with
  NOT and ESCAPE, BETWEEN, IN, COLLATE and CASE.
  - **Not wrapped:** atoms and forms with their own parentheses (names,
  literals, parameters, calls, CAST, subqueries, EXISTS, row values).
  - **Paren nodes:** kept only around an atom, so printing the printed text
  gives the same text.
  - **AS** is added before an alias, which makes `1_000` read as `1 AS _000`
  visible.
  - **Kept as written:**
    - `LIMIT a, b`, because SQLite numbers anonymous `?` in text order.
    - DEFAULT's form, and a comma between table constraints, because a comma
  drops a `CONSTRAINT name`.
    - A declared type keeps its source spacing. SQLite strips a trailing ALWAYS
  only from a type text of 16 bytes or more.
  - **Coverage guard:** `checkPrinted` requires every token to be printed once,
  or deliberately left out.

  Where no parentheses can go, so the check is weaker:
  - the grouping of joins in FROM
  - the parts of a compound SELECT
  - `IN (SELECT)` against `IN ((SELECT))`
  - an operation that starts with the name WITH, which SQLite reads as a keyword
  after `(` (a fuzz finding)
  - the boundary between a column's type and its constraints, where a wrong tree
  prints the same tokens; no text comparison can see it, so the parser's pinned
  tests must cover it
  - NOT BETWEEN, NOT LIKE and NOT IN against NOT outside, which SQLite lowers to
  the same tree

  ## How statements without a schema are compared

  - **Schema:** guessed from the original tree, then fixed one SQLite error at a
  time, up to 40 times.
    - It takes tables and views, parent tables of foreign keys, and columns.
  Every unqualified column starts on the first table.
    - Columns get types and collations by a hash of their name, so affinity and
  collation show in the bytecode.
    - The same schema serves both texts, so a wrong guess can cause a skip or a
  fault, never two different trees looking the same.
  - **Bytecode:** `EXPLAIN` opcode, P1–P5 and comment, without the address.
  Three masked fields, all text SQLite copies:
    - Trace P4 (trigger step text)
    - the Halt P4 of an unnamed CHECK (a named one keeps its name, so
  `CONSTRAINT name` attachment is still compared)
    - the `vtab:` memory address
  - **DDL ("probes"):** the statement is executed, then the PRAGMAs are
  compared, plus the programs of INSERT, UPDATE and DELETE, and a SELECT of
  comparisons on every column.
  - **Expressions:** each is also evaluated on its own over a one-row table of
  64 typed columns, for 3 sets of values. The result is compared as `typeof`,
  `quote`, and comparisons that show affinity and collation, plus its bytecode.

  ## Coverage on the corpus

  Of 83,991 statements checked:
  - 72,383 (86.2%) compared by bytecode or probes
  - 4,551 (5.4%) compared only by an error; 2,306 of these are CREATE VIRTUAL
  TABLE, whose arguments are just tokens
  - the remaining 7,057 failed or were skipped as statements but had expressions
  compared on their own
  - the rest of the 139,234 texts: 55,232 do not parse, 11 SQLite does not say

  Reasons for "same error" are mostly functions or modules the binding lacks
  (snippet, offsets, REGEXP), sqlite_stat1/4, and errors no schema avoids.

  Of 74,878 distinct expressions, 57,994 were compared by bytecode and 53,390 by
  values; values were skipped for 13,195 that read tables. The per-kind and
  per-form tables are in the README.

  ## Pairs and triples

  - **Pairs:** 61 forms, all 3,721 ordered pairs, each in every operand, 3 times
  (21,594 texts).
    - 3,084 pairs compared by bytecode and values, 198 by bytecode only.
    - 439 only by an identical error: all involve REGEXP, MATCH, GLOB with
  ESCAPE, or a misused row value. The binding has no REGEXP or MATCH function.
  - **Triples:** 20,000 texts; 14,333 combinations compared by bytecode and
  values, 1,499 by bytecode only.
  - **Rejected texts:** 222 pairs and 281 triples are rejected by SQLite and by
  the parser alike, with no `parsetest` disagreement.

  ## Detection rate per mutation

  | Mutation | Detected / undetected | Rate |
  |---|---|---|
  | rotate two operators | 2,226 / 69 | 97.0% |
  | swap operands | 4,240 / 130 | 97.0% |
  | NOT of IS NOT moved to its operand | 122 / 0 | 100% |
  | NOT BETWEEN/LIKE/IN moved out | 0 / 650 | 0% (expected; the test fails if it
  is ever detected) |
  | LIMIT operands swapped | 19 / 1 | 95% |
  | ON or USING to the next join | 6 / 1 | 86% |
  | join operators swapped | 140 / 10 | 93% |
  | ORDER BY or LIMIT moved | 33 / 0 | 100% |
  | ELSE of a nested CASE | 1 / 0 | 100% |
  | FILTER or OVER to an argument | 3 / 0 | 100% |
  | ORDER BY between arguments and window | 83 / 0 | 100% |
  | upsert WHEREs swapped | 7 / 0 | 100% |
  | column constraint moved | 146 / 9 | 94% |
  | CONSTRAINT name moved | 1 / 2 | 33% |
  | digit-separator split | 2 / 0 | 100% |

  Every undetected class is one SQLite treats the same:
  - unary `+` compiles to nothing
  - AND and OR are associative, and SQLite splits WHERE into AND terms
  - COLLATE over a numeric result, or COLLATE BINARY
  - `x OR <true constant>` and `x AND 0` are folded, and `x IN ()` is constant
  false
  - a NULL test of a literal is folded at parse time
  - swaps of equal operands, or of symmetric operators
  - an ON moved to an earlier inner join (SQLite treats it as a WHERE term)
  - outer joins that the WHERE turns into inner joins
  - a column CHECK moved (every CHECK is on the table)
  - a NULL constraint moved (it means nothing)
  - a CONSTRAINT name moved off a UNIQUE or PRIMARY KEY (SQLite keeps only CHECK
  names)

  ## Faults found in the parser's tree

  None, so the parser is unchanged.

  ## Fuzzing

  Seven runs with 6 workers, about 313 million executions in total; the last run
  made 123,112,704 in 30 minutes and found nothing. The other six runs each
  found one fault in the check, never in the parser, and each input is now a
  regression case in `testdata/fuzz/FuzzCheck`:
  - a comment inside a declared type is part of the type
  - two CHECK texts that print alike got different masks
  - three findings where the printed text's extra nesting hits a depth limit
  - the WITH case above

  ## Run times

  The machine ran at a load of 15 to 45 on 10 cores from other sessions, so
  treat these as upper bounds.
  - **Plain:** about 15 s, 53 s of CPU, because the corpus and pairs run in 8
  child processes. SQLite serializes allocations across threads, so goroutines
  did not help. It took up to 82 s under heavy load.
  - **`-race`:** about 26 s, one process, every tenth corpus entry.
  - **Address sanitizer:** about 42 s in Docker, plus 30 s of `make fuzz`. `make
  test-asan` passed in 756 s at load 30.

  ## Where SQLite surprised me

  - The ALWAYS rule above: whether the word is stripped depends on the length of
  the type text.
  - A comment inside a type is part of the type and can change its affinity.
  - The text of an unnamed CHECK goes into the program, and a comma after
  `CONSTRAINT name` drops the name.
  - An action error such as `IN(...) element has 3 terms - expected 2` can be
  raised at an added `)`. The check accepts that when the message matches the
  original's.
  - With foreign keys on by default, probes failed until parent tables existed.
  - In the corpus, `CAST(zeroblob(1E9) AS INT)` made a 1 GB value, which got the
  sanitizer run killed until I added the length limit.

  ## What I'm unsure of

  - **REGEXP and GLOB with ESCAPE** are compared only by their error, because
  the binding cannot register functions. MATCH is compared by bytecode but not
  by values. Adding function registration to the binding would close this.
  - **Column names:** five corpus statements refer to a column that SQLite names
  by the expression's text, which the printer changes, so they are skipped.
  - **CI cost:** the plain test adds about 15–30 s, using 8 processes. I have
  not measured it on CI itself.
  - **Sharding:** the README and the code comments say the fixed 8 shards make
  the report the same on every machine. The tests check only that there are no
  faults, not that the report is stable.

  Created PR #26, ran 1 shell command (ctrl+o to expand)

⏺ The shape check is built and found no fault in the parser's tree. It is in
  pull request #26, waiting on CI.

  - What it does: writes each tree out with every operation in parentheses,
    creates the tables and columns the statement names, and requires that SQLite
    produces the same bytecode and the same values as for the original text.
  - Coverage on the corpus: of the 83,991 statements that parse, 86% were
    compared by bytecode or by their effect on the schema. Another 5% were
    compared only by getting the same error, mostly because our binding lacks a
    function or module they use.
  - Operators: all 3,721 ordered pairs of 61 operator forms and 20,000 triples,
    plus 313 million fuzz executions. No fault in the tree.
  - The check can fail: trees damaged on purpose in 15 ways are caught, mostly
    at 93–100%. What it misses is what SQLite itself treats as the same program,
    such as regrouping ANDs. Two kinds are missed by design for that reason:
    NOT x LIKE y against x NOT LIKE y, and a moved constraint name, which SQLite
    keeps only for CHECK.

  Three weaker spots remain:

  - Column type against constraints: where a column's type ends and its
    constraints begin cannot be seen this way, because both readings print the
    same text. I plan a small follow-up that compares our tree's column types
    directly with what SQLite reports for the created table.
  - REGEXP and MATCH: compared only by their error, because our binding
    registers no functions. The same follow-up adds stub functions so they are
    compared by bytecode.
  - Joins and compound selects: their grouping cannot be parenthesized, so there
    the check rests on the structural printing; moved ON clauses and swapped
    join operators are caught at 86–93%.

  Usage is at 21% or a little more against the 30% budget.

✻ Crunched for 9s · done Monday 8:54 PM

› Message from fabrik:builder
  ## Shape check weak spots: both closed, no fault found in the parser's tree

  Both weak spots are closed on the branch **`shape-check-weak-spots`** (7
  commits on `e485b60`, not pushed). The new check found **no fault in the
  parser's tree**: not on the corpus, the pairs and triples, the hand-written
  statements, or about 95 million fuzz executions. So the parser is unchanged
  and there are no new pinned parser tests. `make lint`, `make test` and `make
  test-asan FUZZTIME=30s` pass, and the new tests pass under `-race` and 50
  times in a row.

  ## Weak spot 1: what the tree declares

  `shapetest.Check` now makes a second comparison that does not use the printed
  text. It is a model of `build.c` in `internal/sql/shapetest/declared.go`, and
  it stays in the test package.
  - **How it runs:** it replays the actions of `parse.y` over the tree in order.
  It runs on the connection where the original text ran, and compares the tree
  it is given (the damaged tree in the mutation test).
  - **Where it shows up:** `Result.Declared` and `Report.Declared` (a new
  "declared" column). A difference is a fault starting "the tree declares what
  SQLite does not make of the text: ".
  - **Statements covered:** CREATE TABLE (not AS), ALTER TABLE ... ADD COLUMN
  and ADD CHECK, CREATE INDEX, and CREATE VIEW with a column list.

  What is compared, and where SQLite's answer comes from:
  - **Each column** (name, type text, not null, default text exactly,
  primary-key position, hidden 2 or 3): `pragma table_xinfo`.
  - **Collation of each column:** no PRAGMA gives it, so the check makes an
  index on every column under a name the schema does not have, reads `pragma
  index_xinfo`, and drops it.
  - **WITHOUT ROWID, STRICT, number of columns:** `pragma table_list`.
  - **AUTOINCREMENT:** whether `sqlite_sequence` exists in the table's schema.
  The guessed schema never makes an AUTOINCREMENT table.
  - **Foreign keys:** `pragma foreign_key_list`.
  - **Deferral of each foreign key:** no PRAGMA gives it. The program of `insert
  into t default values` has an `FkCounter` whose P1 is the deferral, or a
  `Halt` 787 for an immediate one, in the PRAGMA's order. It is not compared for
  a table that refers to itself.
  - **Indexes from UNIQUE and PRIMARY KEY:** `pragma index_list` and the key
  rows of `index_xinfo`, compared as a sorted set.
  - **CHECK names, in order:** the `Halt` 275 instructions of the same program,
  which carry the name in P4.
  - **CREATE INDEX:** the new index against `index_xinfo` and `index_list`.
  - **CREATE VIEW:** the column names against `table_xinfo`. Skipped when two
  names differ only by case, because SQLite then adds `:N`, randomly after 3
  collisions.

  SQLite rules I had to model, with their sources:
  - **`sqlite3AddColumn`:**
    - The type is the source text from the first type token to the last.
    - A type of 16 bytes or more loses a trailing ALWAYS, then a GENERATED
  before it.
    - `sqlite3DequoteToken` takes quotes off and drops the last byte whatever it
  is, so `[int] x` becomes `int] `.
    - The standard types are shown upper case.
    - Otherwise `sqlite3Dequote` keeps only the first quoted part, so `'abc'
  def` becomes `abc`.
    - Adding a column ends a CONSTRAINT name.
  - **`sqlite3AddDefaultValue`** with `sqlite3DbSpanDup`: the default's text by
  its form, trimmed. The last DEFAULT counts.
  - **`sqlite3AddPrimaryKey`:** a single INTEGER column is the rowid unless a
  column constraint says DESC. A table constraint passes order 0, so `primary
  key(a desc)` is still the rowid. Strings are names (`sqlite3StringToId`).
  - **`sqlite3CreateIndex`:** a key with the same columns and collations as an
  earlier one makes no new index, and a PRIMARY KEY takes over the earlier
  index.
  - **`sqlite3AddCollateType`:** it also fixes the indexes already made on that
  column.
  - **`sqlite3AddCheckConstraint`:** a CONSTRAINT name is not used up. It names
  every later CHECK until the next column or a `tconscomma`. The comma before
  the first table constraint is not a `tconscomma`, so a name at the end of the
  last column names the first table CHECK.
  - **`sqlite3AddGenerated`:** VIRTUAL unless STORED.
  - **`sqlite3CreateForeignKey` and `refargs`:** the last ON DELETE and the last
  ON UPDATE count, and MATCH is dropped, so the PRAGMA always says NONE.
  - **`sqlite3DeferForeignKey`:** DEFERRABLE applies to the foreign key made
  last, of any earlier column.
  - **`sqlite3EndTable` and `convertToWithoutRowidTable`:** STRICT and WITHOUT
  ROWID add NOT NULL to key columns, WITHOUT ROWID turns the rowid back into an
  index, and repeated key columns are removed.
  - **`pragma table_xinfo` (`pragma.c`):** the primary-key position is capped at
  nCol+1, because the PRAGMA only looks at the first nCol entries.

  Other places where a wrong tree prints the same tokens: there are none outside
  column definitions. CREATE INDEX and CREATE VIEW have no ambiguity that keeps
  the token order, but they are compared anyway because it costs little.

  **Mutations** (detected / undetected / not compared; the last number is how
  many only the new comparison caught):

  | Damage | Result | Only by declarations |
  | --- | --- | ---: |
  | First constraint of a column moved into its type | 773 / 0 / 17 | 649 |
  | Last word of a type moved into AS | 3 / 0 / 0 | 3 |
  | Form of DEFAULT changed (name as a column, sign as an operator) | 29 / 0 / 0
  | 9 |
  | ON CONFLICT moved to the next constraint | 3 / 0 / 0 | 0 |
  | ON DELETE and ON UPDATE swapped | 4 / 0 / 0 | 0 |
  | A column constraint moved to the next column | 494 / 16 / 14 (96.9%) | 1 |
  | CONSTRAINT name moved, table constraints | 5 / 6 / 0 | 0 |
  | CONSTRAINT name moved, in a column | 2 / 4 / 2 | 0 |
  | GENERATED ALWAYS moved from the type into AS | 0 / 13 | expected: SQLite
  drops those words; the test fails if it is ever detected |

  - **Column constraint moved:** COLLATE (15 detected) and DEFAULT (28 detected)
  were never missed.
  - **Re-measured on the old sample:** the two rows you asked about are
  unchanged, 146/9/3 and 1/2, with none caught only by declarations. Both
  damages change the token order, so the old comparison already saw what SQLite
  sees.
  - **Every miss is a case SQLite records the same:**
    - a CHECK moved to another column, because SQLite keeps every CHECK on the
  table;
    - a NULL constraint moved;
    - a DEFERRABLE moved to a column without REFERENCES, which still applies to
  the same foreign key;
    - a CONSTRAINT name on anything but a CHECK, which SQLite does not keep;
    - GENERATED ALWAYS, by design.

  ## Weak spot 2: REGEXP, MATCH, GLOB ... ESCAPE

  Each failed for a different reason:
  - SQLite has no `regexp` function: "no such function: regexp".
  - Its `match` is a placeholder for virtual tables that fails when it runs:
  "unable to use function MATCH in the requested context".
  - Its `glob` takes two arguments, but `X GLOB Y ESCAPE Z` calls it with three:
  "wrong number of arguments to function glob()".

  **New binding function:** `sqlite.Conn.AddTestFunctions()`. It registers
  static C stubs in the binding's own C code: `regexp` and `match` with 2 or 3
  arguments, and `glob` with 3. They are deterministic and innocuous, and the
  two-argument built-in glob stays.
  - **Behaviour:** NULL if any argument is NULL. Otherwise -1, 0 or 1 for the
  byte order of the first argument's text against the second's, a prefix first.
  With a third argument it is 3 times that plus the order of the second to the
  third. Note that `X REGEXP Y` calls `regexp(Y, X)`.
  - **Where it is used:** documented as for tests' connections only, and
  `shapetest` calls it on every connection it opens.
  - **Tests:** `TestConn_AddTestFunctions`, which also runs under the sanitizer.
  - **I added the 3-argument `glob` beyond what you asked for**, so that GLOB
  ... ESCAPE is compared too.

  | | Before | Now |
  | --- | ---: | ---: |
  | Pairs compared by bytecode and values | 3,084 | 3,610 of 3,721 |
  | Pairs compared by bytecode only | 198 | 0 |
  | Pairs compared only by an error | 439 | 111 |
  | Triple combinations compared only by an error | – | 783 of 19,100 |

  - **What is left:** all 111 pairs and all 783 triple combinations hold a row
  value where SQLite needs a single value. Its errors are "row value misused",
  "IN(...) element has 1 term - expected 2" and "sub-select returns 1 columns -
  expected 2". No function avoids them.
  - **Corpus:** REGEXP is now compared by values in 153 of 154 expressions (was
  0), and MATCH in 1,748 of 1,791 (was 0). Statements compared by bytecode or
  probes went from 72,383 to 72,510. "no such function: REGEXP" (119 statements)
  is gone from the errors.
  - **Corpus expressions with these operators still not compared** fail for
  other reasons: a function the binding lacks, a table they name, a row value,
  or an ESCAPE that is not one character.

  ## Fuzzing and run times

  - **Fuzzing:** with 6 workers, 35,737,982 executions in 20 minutes before the
  review fixes, then 59,494,380 in 30 minutes on the final code. No finding, so
  no new regression cases. `FuzzCheck` is also seeded with `Declared`.
  - **Run times:** the machine was at load 40 to 65 from other sessions, so wall
  times say little. Back to back with the code on `main`:

  | Run | Before | After |
  | --- | --- | --- |
  | Plain | 52 s, 62 s CPU | 39 s, 65 s CPU |
  | `-race` | 79 s, 45 s CPU | 116 s, 49 s CPU |

  - Under the sanitizer the package takes 85 s (the earlier 42 s was at load
  about 15), and `make test-asan` took 790 s in total.
  - `TestCheck_declared` takes about 0.3 s, and `TestCheck_mutations` about 11
  s.

  ## Self-review

  The code-review pass found ten points. Two would have reported a fault where
  the tree is right, and I fixed both:
  - The probe index name collided with statements that use it, such as `create
  table s4ql_collations(a)`.
  - A `create view if not exists v(x) ...` over a name the guessed schema
  already holds compared against the stand-in table.

  I also fixed the rest: the table is now found in the schema the statement
  names, the mutation count no longer counts declarations for unrelated damage,
  the long summary line is guarded again, a repeated PRAGMA query is gone, and
  the duplicated index logic is one function. The writer then pointed out that
  SQLite refuses `rowid` in any index, so I removed that unreachable branch.

  ## Surprises from SQLite and things I am unsure of

  Besides the rules above:
  - An index cannot name the rowid at all.
  - `x REGEXP y >= 0` means `x REGEXP (y >= 0)`.

  Unsure:
  - Reading deferral and CHECK names from the INSERT program depends on how
  SQLite emits it (`fkLookupParent`, `sqlite3GenerateConstraintChecks`). It
  holds on the corpus and the fuzzer, but it is not a PRAGMA.
  - AUTOINCREMENT is detected per schema. That is only right because the guessed
  schema never makes an AUTOINCREMENT table, which a comment now says.
  - The `glob`/3 stub is my addition; drop it if you want only `regexp` and
  `match`.

  ## Docs

  - `internal/sql/shapetest/README.md` (written by a writer sub-agent, which I
  reviewed): new section "What the tree declares", and new numbers in every
  table.
  - `internal/sqlite/README.md`: one bullet and one sentence.
  - `docs/todo.md`: the line on the shape check's blind spots was wrong and is
  fixed.
  - Diary at `docs/diary/2026-11-16-shape-check-weak-spots.md`, steps 1 to 5.
